Kumar Bhardwaj, [(2021) 18 SCC 104], more
particularly paragraphs 24 and 28, which are
reproduced hereunder for ease of reference:
24. Mr. Khan further raised an argument that the
final notification under Section 20 of the Forest Act
has not been published. A reading of Section 20 of
the Forest Act does not show that for a reserved
forest, there is a requirement of publication of
notification but no time limit is prescribed for
publication of such notification under Section 20.
Therefore, even if notification under Section
20 of the Forest Act has not been issued, by
virtue of Section 5 of the Forest Act, there is a
prohibition against acquisition of any right
over the land comprised in such notification
except by way of a contract executed in
writing by or on behalf of the Government.
Since no such written contract was executed by or
on behalf of the State or on behalf of the person in
whom such right was vested, therefore, the Gaon
Sabha was not competent to grant lease in favour of
the appellant.
xxx
28. The High Court had referred to the objections
filed by the lessees under the Consolidation Act and
also objections by the Forest Department. It was
held by the High Court that since no objections were
filed by the Forest Department earlier, therefore,
the objections would be barred by Section 49 of the
Consolidation Act. We find that such finding
recorded by the High Court is clearly erroneous.
The land vests in the Forest Department by
virtue of notification published under a
statute. It was the lessee who had to assert
the title on the forest land by virtue of an
agreement in writing by a competent authority
but no such agreement in writing has been
produced. Therefore, the lessee would not be

- 183 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
entitled to any right only on the basis of an
entry in the revenue record.
65.74. By relying on Arun Kumar Bhardwaj, Learned
Additional Advocate General submits that in
paragraph 24, the Hon’ble Supreme Court
observed that, notwithstanding the absence of
a final notification under Section 20 of the
applicable Forest Act, the prohibition under
Section 5 against acquisition of rights over land
comprised in the preliminary notification
continued to operate, except where such rights
were acquired under a written contract
executed by or on behalf of the Government or
a person competent to create such rights. The
Court consequently held that the Gaon Sabha
was not competent to grant the lease in
question in the absence of such a written
contract.
65.75. In paragraph 28, the Hon’ble Supreme Court
further held that the lessee could not claim
rights over the forest land merely on the basis
of an entry in the revenue records, particularly
when no written agreement executed by a

- 184 -
 HC-KAR

competent authority had been produced to
establish the claimed title.
65.76. Placing reliance on the aforesaid judgment, the
learned Additional Advocate General submits
that the absence of a final notification under
the applicable forest legislation does not, by
itself, render every subsequent grant or
transaction concerning land covered by a
preliminary forest notification legally valid. He
contends that the legal effect of any grant,
lease or other transaction relied upon by the
Petitioners must be examined with reference to
the applicable statutory restrictions and the
competence of the authority purporting to
create the right. He further submits that
revenue entries, by themselves, cannot confer
title where the claimed right is not supported
by a legally valid grant or other instrument
recognised by law.
65.77. Accordingly, he contends that the Petitioners
cannot rely solely upon revenue entries or
subsequent transactions to establish an
enforceable right over the lands in question
without demonstrating that the rights asserted

lawful action affecting them. On this basis, he

have been occupied, developed or used for
commercial purposes. He submits that the
Forest Department’s efforts to protect
Moorkangudda must therefore be considered in
the light of the ecological functions performed
by the shola forests and grasslands and the
wider public interest in conserving the natural
environment.
65.110. Learned Additional Advocate General finally
submits that the ecological significance of
Moorkangudda reinforces the need to preserve
the area and to prevent further degradation
pending determination of the competing claims.
He contends that the protection of the shola
forests and grasslands is a matter of long-term
environmental security and that the
consequences of irreversible ecological damage
may extend to biodiversity, catchment stability
and the availability of water resources. He
accordingly urges that the claims of individual
occupants and the legality of the activities
undertaken in the subject lands be examined in
a manner that gives due consideration to the
ecological sensitivity of the area and the State’s
obligation to protect the environment.

65.111. Turning to the statutory framework, learned
counsel submits that the preliminary
notification dated 05.02.1920 was issued under
Section 4 of the Mysore Forest Regulation,
1900, declaring the Government’s proposal to
constitute the notified area as a State Forest
and initiating the statutory process for the
ascertainment and settlement of rights claimed
in respect of the area. He contends that the
Regulation does not prescribe an express outer
time limit within which the final notification
under Section 17 must be published. On that
basis, he argues that the absence of a final
notification, merely because a considerable
period has elapsed since the preliminary
notification, should not automatically result in
the extinguishment of the State’s authority to
protect the notified lands.
65.112. He further submits that the preliminary
notification was not a mere declaration of an
intention without legal consequences. According
to him, the notification attracted the statutory
restrictions applicable to the notified area,
including restrictions upon the acquisition of
rights after its publication, subject to the

exceptions recognised by law. He therefore

made in revenue records, grants or other

65.113. Learned Additional Advocate General further

of delay, laches and acquiescence. According to

paragraphs 8, 10-17, and 19 to thereof, which

8. Any action taken by the authorities prior to the
issuance of the impugned notification is irrelevant
for the purpose of the case. The notification dated
22-6-1991 states that an extent of 422.32 hectares
in Uchangi Durga Village, Davangere District, is
notified as reserved forest. The said notification has
appointed the Range Forest Officer as the Forest
Settlement Officer. Once any land is notified to
constitute as a reserved forest, various steps are
contemplated under the Act before a declaration
can be issued under Section 17 of the Act declaring
that the land is reserved forest. The relevant
provisions of the Act in this regard are extracted
below for felicity of reference:
“2. Definitions. —In this Act, unless the context
otherwise requires.—
(14) “Reserved Forest” means any land
settled and notified as such in accordance
with the provisions of Chapter II of this Act;
3. Powers to constitute reserved forests.— The
State Government may constitute any land which is
the property of the Government or over which the
Government has proprietary rights, or to the whole,
or any part of the forest produce of which the
Government is entitled, a reserved forest in the
manner hereinafter provided.
4. Notification by Government.—(1) Whenever it
has been decided to constitute any land a reserved
forest the State Government shall issue a
notification.—
(a) declaring that it has been decided to constitute
such land a reserved forest;

(b) specifying, as nearly as possible, the situation
and limits of such land; and
(c) appointing an officer (hereinafter called the
“Forest Settlement Officer”) to inquire into and
determine the existence, nature and extent of any
rights claimed by or alleged to exist in favour of any
person in or over any land comprised within such
limits or in or over any forest produce, and to deal
with the same as provided in this Chapter.
Explanation. —For the purpose of clause (b) it shall
be sufficient to describe the limits of the forest-by
roads, rivers, bridges, or other well-known or
readily intelligible boundaries.
(2) The officer appointed under clause (c) of sub-section (1) shall, be a person not holding any forest
office except that of Forest Settlement Officer; but
a Forest Officer may be appointed by the State
Government to represent it in the inquiry under this
Chapter by the Forest Settlement Officer.
5. Proclamation by Forest Settlement Officer.
—When a notification has been issued under
Section 4, the Forest Settlement Officer shall
publish in Kannada and in any other regional
language of the area, at the Headquarters of” each
taluk in which any portion of the land comprised in
such notification is situate and in every town and
village in the neighbourhood of such land a
proclamation.—
(a) specifying, as nearly as possible, the situation
and limits of the proposed forest;
(b) setting forth the substance of the provisions of
Section 6;
(c) explaining the consequences which, as
hereinafter provided, will ensue on such forest
being constituted a reserved forest; and
(d) fixing a period of not less than three months
from the date of publishing such proclamation, and

requiring every person claiming any right or making
any claim referred to or mentioned in Section 4,
either to present to such officer within such period
a written notice specifying or to appear before him
within such period and state the nature of such
right or claim (if any) and in either case to produce
all documents in support thereof.
The Forest Settlement Officer shall also serve a
notice to the same effect on every known or
reputed owner or occupier of any land included in
or adjoining the land proposed” to be constituted a
reserved forest or on his recognised agent or
manager. Such notice may be sent by registered
post to persons residing beyond the limits of the
district in which such land is situate.
6. Bar of accrual of forest rights.—(1) After the
issue of a notification under Section 4, no right shall
be acquired in or over the land comprised in such
notification, except by succession or under a grant
or contract in writing made or entered into by or on
behalf of the Government or some person in whom
such right, or power to create such right, was
vested when the notification was issued; and on
such land no new house shall be built or plantation
formed, no fresh clearings for cultivation or for any
other purpose shall be made and no trees shall be
cut for the purpose of trade or manufacture except
as hereinafter provided. No patta or right of
occupancy shall without the previous sanction of
the State Government be granted, in respect of
such land, and every patta or right of occupancy
granted without such sanction shall be null and
void.
(2) Nothing in this section shall be deemed to
prohibit any act done under the written permission
of the Forest Settlement Officer.
(3) No Civil Court shall, between the dates of
publication of the notification under Section 4 and
of the final notification to be issued under Section
17 entertain any suit to establish any right in or

over any land or to the forest produce of any land
included in the notification under Section 4.
7. Inquiry by Forest Settlement Officer.— The
Forest Settlement Officer shall take down in writing
all statements made under clause (d) of Section 5
and shall, at some convenient place, inquire into all
claims duly preferred under that section and into
the existence and extent of any rights mentioned in
Section 4 and not claimed under Section 5 so far as
the same may be ascertainable from the records of
the Government and the evidence of any person
likely to be acquainted with the same.
The Forest Settlement Officer shall at the same
time, consider and record any objection which the
Forest Officer, if any, appointed under sub-section
(2) of Section 4 may make to any such claim or any
information which he may afford with regard to the
existence and extent of any such right.
8. Powers of Forest Settlement Officer.— For
the purpose of such inquiry, the Forest Settlement
Officer may exercise the following powers, that is to
say.—
(i) power to enter, by himself or any officer
authorised by him for the purpose, upon any land,
and to survey, demarcate and make a map of the
same; and
(ii) the powers of a Civil Court in the trial of suits.
9. Extinction of rights— Rights in respect of
which no claim has been preferred under Section 5,
and of the existence of which no knowledge has
been acquired by inquiry under Section 7, shall be
extinguished unless, before the final notification
under Section 17 is published, the person claiming
them satisfies the Forest Settlement Officer that he
had sufficient cause for not preferring such claim
within the period fixed under Section 5 in which
case the Forest Settlement Officer shall proceed to
dispose of the claim as hereinafter provided.

xxx
17. Notification declaring forest a reserved forest.—
(1) When the following events have occurred,
namely.—
(a) the period fixed under Section 5 for preferring
claims has elapsed, and all claims, if any, made
under that section or Section 9 have been disposed
of by the Forest Settlement Officer;
(b) if any such claims have been made, the period
limited by Section 16 for appealing from the orders
passed on such claims has elapsed and all appeals
(if any) presented within such period have been
disposed of; and
(c) all proceedings prescribed by Sections 11 and
14 have been taken and all lands and buildings (if
any) to be included in the proposed reserved forest,
which the Forest Settlement Officer has under
Section 11 elected to acquire under the Land
Acquisition Act, 1894, have become vested in the
Government under Section 16 of that Act,
the State Government shall publish a notification
specifying clearly according to the boundary marks
erected or otherwise, the limits of the forest which
is intended to constitute a reserved forest and
declaring the same to be a reserved forest from the
date fixed by such notification, subject to the
exercise of rights (if any) specified in such
notification.
(2) From the date so fixed, such forest shall be
deemed to be a reserved forest”.
10. The notification issued under Section 4 of the
Act may be termed as a Preliminary Notification.
The same has to be published by the Forest
Settlement Officer under Section 5 as a
proclamation and also a copy of the notice has to
be served on every known owner or occupier of any
land included in or adjoining the land proposed to
be constituted a reserved forest or on his

recognized agent or manager. On the issuance of a
notification under Section 4, no right can be
acquired in or over the land comprised in the
notification, except by succession or under a grant
or contract in writing made or entered into by or on
behalf of the Government. Also no Civil Court can
entertain any suit for the establishment of any right
on any land included in the notification issued under
Section 4.
11. Under clause (d) of Section 5 of the Act, under
which a proclamation is made by the Forest
Settlement Officer a period of three months from
the date of publishing the proclamation is given to
any person claiming any right or making any claim
referred to under Section 4 by producing all
documents in support thereof. If such a claim is
made, the Forest Settlement Officer has to enquire
into the claim by looking into the records of the
Government and the evidence of any person
acquainted with the same and also hear the
concerned Forest Officer as regards any objections
under Section 7.
12. Section 8 grants certain powers to the Forest
Settlement Officer for the purpose of conducting an
enquiry. If no claim is made within the period fixed
under Section 5 before the Forest Officer, any
rights in that regard shall be extinguished unless
before the final notification under Section 17 is
published, the person claiming them satisfies the
Forest Settlement Officer that he had sufficient
cause for not preferring such a claim within the
period fixed under Section 5 in which case the
Forest Settlement Officer would have to proceed to
dispose of the claim.
13. Section 10 deals with the manner in which
claims relating to shifting cultivation would have to
be dealt with. Section 11 enables the Forest
Settlement Officer to acquire land over which a
right is claimed in case such land is not excluded
from the limits of the reserved forest or no
agreement with the owner for the surrender of his

- 219 -

AND 3 OTHERS
rights is arrived at. In the event of an acquisition,
the provisions of the Land Acquisition Act, 1894
would apply and the Forest Settlement Officer
would act as a Deputy Commissioner. With regard
to the right of way to pasturage or other forest
produce or water, the Forest Settlement Officer has
to consider any such claims and pass an order
under Section 12 read with Sections 13 and 14. If
no settlement under Section 14 is arrived at, then
compensation has to be determined on the basis of
the value of the right claimed on the date of the
preliminary notification in accordance with the
provisions of the Act, insofar as such provisions are
applicable under Section 15 of the Act.
14. Section 16 provides for an appeal against an
order passed under Section 11, 12, 14 or 15. The
statutory period for filing of an appeal is three
months from the date of the order made by the
Forest Settlement Officer. Section 17 states that
when the period fixed under Section 5 for preferring
a claim has lapsed and all claims have been
disposed of under Section 9 and the period of
limitation for filing an appeal under Section 16 has
lapsed and appeals if any filed have been disposed
of and all proceedings under Sections 11 and 14
have been taken, the State Government shall
publish a final notification specifying clearly the
boundary marks of the reserved forest and declare
the same to be a reserved forest from the date
fixed by a notification subject to the exercise of
rights if any specified in such a notification. From
the date so fixed, such forest shall be deemed to be
a reserved forest. Before the date fixed in the
notification under Section 18, the same shall be
published in the Official Gazette and at the Taluk
Headquarters in which the forest is situated and in
the neighbourhood as a proclamation is published
under Section 5.
15. On a reading of the aforesaid provisions, it
becomes clear that though no specific date has
been fixed for the issuance of a declaration under
Section 17 of the Act, nevertheless, after the

- 220 -
 HC-KAR

issuance of the preliminary notification, a period of
three months is granted to every person to make a
claim. Also, the provisions of the Land Acquisition
Act, 1894 would have to be read into Section 11 of
the Act in case, a necessity to acquire the land
arises.
16. It is observed that in many provisions of the
Land Acquisition Act, 1894, specified time-frames
have been mandated-from the date of publication
of the preliminary and final notification as well as
for passing of an award. No doubt, an elaborate
procedure has been contemplated between the
issuance of a preliminary notification and the
issuance of a declaration or final notification under
the Act under consideration encompassing the
making of claims with regard to the notified areas
proposed to be declared to be reserved forest and
also settlement of the said claims, but the
provisions of the Land Acquisition Act, 1894, are
also contemplated and orders passed under
Sections 11, 12, 14 and 15 are also subject to
appeal. However, there is no specified period
mentioned under the Act within which, the
notification declaring the reserved forest has to be
issued under Section 17 of the Act, once the
preliminary notification has been issued.
17. Before answering the contention raised by the
Petitioners that in the absence of any declaration
under Section 17 of the Act, the lands notified
under Section 4 of the Act cannot become forest
lands and therefore, FC clearance is not required, it
would be necessary to first set at rest, the rival
contentions raised on the basis of the decision of
the Division Bench of this Court in V. Sampangi
Ramaiah's case to which one of us (Mrs. B.V.
Nagarathna, J.) was a member. Briefly stated, the
facts of that case were that the Petitioner therein
was granted quarrying lease in respect of 12.30
acres of land in Sy. No. 601/A1 in Kollegal Taluk.
Notice dated 8-7-2005 was issued to the Petitioner
therein calling upon him to stop all quarrying
operations. The same was assailed in W.P. No.

17941 of 2005, which was allowed by this Court by
order dated 16-8-2007 and the impugned notice
was quashed. As preliminary notification dated 3-6-
1991 had been issued under Section 4(1) of the
Act, and the Assistant Commissioner of Kollegal
Station was appointed as Forest Settlement Officer
by the State Government to enquire into the
existence of any rights or claims of persons under
the Act pursuant to Section 4 notification, a
communication dated 18-1-2007 was issued to the
Petitioner therein. In the meeting held on 20-1-
2007 under the Chairmanship of the Forest
Settlement Officer-cum-Assistant Commissioner, it
was decided to exclude 366.63 acres of land and
the balance 617.71 acres was to be constituted as
reserved forest out of a total extent of 984.34 acres
in Sy. No. 601/A1. When the matter stood thus,
another notice dated 17-10-2008 was issued to the
Petitioner therein asking him to stop all quarrying
operations and not to transport the quarried
material till further orders. That notice was assailed
in W.P. No. 13708 of 2008 and by order dated 25-
6-2009, the writ petition was allowed and the
notice was quashed reserving liberty to the Joint
Director, Department of Mines and Geology, Mysore
Zone to hold a detailed enquiry with regard to the
nature of the land and to pass orders in accordance
with law. During the pendency of that writ petition,
the lease period had come to an end and an
application for renewal had been filed by the
Petitioner therein seeking renewal of 10 acres of
land out of 12.30 acres of land granted originally.
By an endorsement dated 8-1-2010, the Petitioner
therein was informed by an endorsement that the
land sought by him for renewal of the lease was
notified as a reserved forest. Assailing that
endorsement a revision petition was filed, which
was dismissed and thereafter, the writ petition was
filed. In the order passed in the writ petition,
paragraphs 19 to 23 are extracted for immediate
reference:
“19. When specific period has been fixed, for
making a claim and also by filing an appeal by any

- 222 -
 HC-KAR

persons, a corresponding time-frame for the
adjudication of the claims and for issuance of a
declaration ought to have been made. The
absence of any specific time frame for the
issuance of a declaration under Section 17
would imply that such a declaration must be
made within a reasonable time. What is the
reasonable time in the absence of any specific time
frame mentioned in a statutory provision would
vary from Act to Act and depending upon the object
and scope of the Act. In the instant case, having
regard to the object and scope of the Act in
question, we are of the view that a period of
three years would be the reasonable period
within which, a declaration under Section 17
of the Act ought to be made, excluding the
period when steps are taken under the Land
Acquisition Act, 1894, as well as appellate
proceedings, if any. This period obviously would
exclude any stay orders granted by any Court of
Law. But in the instant case, the State is unable to
point out any steps initiated after the order passed
by the Forest Settlement Officer, excluding 366.63
acres, which according to the Petitioner also
includes the area of the subject quarry. This aspect
is communicated to the Petitioner in the year 2007,
whereas in 2004 itself the quarry lease was granted
to the Petitioners.
20. In this context, reliance could be placed on the
fact that originally in the matter of issuance of
preliminary notification and a declaration under the
provisions of the Land Acquisition Act, 1894, there
was no time limit fixed for making an award.
Subsequently, in the year 1984, an amendment
was brought in the form of insertion to Section 11-
A of the Land Acquisition Act. In fact, in the case of
Ram Chand v. Union of India, (1994) 1 SCC 44,
which is a decision on a dispute arising prior to the
insertion of Section 4 to the Land Acquisition Act,
the Supreme Court has held that compensation has
to be paid within a reasonable time, even in the
absence of any time limit prescribed under the Act

- 223 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
and the reasonable time was stated to be about two
years in the said case.
21. In the instant case, though the preliminary
notification is issued on 3-6-1991, the final
notification is yet to be issued. In fact, several
adjournments were given in the matter so as to
enable the Respondent-authorities to take a
decision with regard to issuance of a declaration
under Section 17 of the Act, even then, no steps
have been taken to issue such a declaration.
Therefore, we find that the State Government
has by not taking steps for over two decades
after the issuance of the preliminary
notification, abandoned its intention to notify
Sy. No. 601/A1 as a Reserved Forest. This
becomes all the more clear by the fact that
subsequent to the issuance of the preliminary
notification in the year 1991 and quarrying
lease has also been granted to the Petitioner
and possibly to the other similarly placed
persons in the year 2004. Therefore, the
impugned order at Annexure-A is without authority
of law.
22. The preliminary notification dated 3-6-1991 is
also quashed insofar as Sy. No. 601/A1 is
concerned, though in our view, the entire
preliminary notification is inchoate. Annexures-A
and B are also quashed. The Respondent-Authorities are directed to consider the Petitioner's
application for renewal of the quarrying lease for a
further period of ten years, excluding the period of
lease when the Petitioner was prevented from
carrying on the quarrying activities during the
subsistence of the Quarrying Deed No. 105 since
the year 2004.
23. The writ petition is accordingly, allowed. The
renewal of the lease to be effected within a period
of 30 days from the date of receipt of certified copy
of this order. Parties to bear their respective costs”.
19. Having considered the implication of the
decision in V. Sampangi Ramaiah's case, this case

- 224 -
 HC-KAR

would have to be decided on its facts. If the
provisions of the Act are applied to the facts
of the case, what emerges is that when a
notification is issued under Section 4
declaring the decision to constitute any land
as Reserved Forest, by virtue of Section 6 of
the Act, no contract can be entered into in
respect of that land on behalf of the
Government with any person except with the
previous sanction of the State Government
and no right can be acquired in or around or
over the land comprised in the notification
except by succession or under the grant or
contract in writing made or entered into by or
on behalf of the Government or some person
in whom such right or power to create such
right, was vested when the notification was
issued. No doubt, under Section 6, the State
can enter into a contract with any person in
respect of the lands notified under Section 4
provided the provisions of that section are
fully complied with. Therefore, in that sense the
Petitioners may be partly right in contending that
despite issuance of notification under Section 4 of
the Act State had the power to grant the mining
leases in favour of the Petitioners. But the matter
does not rest here.
65.116. By relying on Girish H.E., the Learned
Additional Advocate General submits that the
issuance of a preliminary notification under
Section 4 of the Karnataka Forest Act, 1963,
initiates the statutory process for constitution of
a reserved forest, which requires further
proceedings in accordance with the provisions
of the Act, culminating in a final notification
under Section 17. He relies on the observations

- 225 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
in paragraphs 8 and 10 to 17 of the said
judgment, which explain the statutory
procedure governing the notification,
publication of proclamation, submission and
inquiry into claims, settlement of rights and
issuance of the final notification.
65.117. The learned Additional Advocate General further
submits that, upon issuance of a notification
under Section 4, the consequences prescribed
under Section 6 of the Act ensue, including
restrictions on the acquisition of rights over the
notified land, subject to the exceptions provided
therein. He contends that the legal effect of
subsequent grants, contracts or other purported
creation of rights must, therefore, be examined
in the light of Section 6 and the circumstances
in which such rights are claimed to have arisen.
65.118. He also relies on the discussion in Girish H.E.
concerning the decision in V. Sampangi
Ramaiah, particularly the principle that,
although the Act does not prescribe a specific
period for issuance of the final notification
under Section 17, the statutory process must
be completed within a reasonable time. He

submits, however, that the question of
reasonable time and the legal consequences of
delay must be determined having regard to the
facts and circumstances of the present case,
including the steps taken by the authorities, the
nature of the land and the rights claimed by the
Petitioners.
65.119. Relying further on paragraph 19 of Girish H.E.,
the learned Additional Advocate General
submits that the effect of a preliminary

without establishing that the rights asserted by

65.120. He relies on B.R. Ganapathi Singh v. State of

particularly Paras 46 and 64 thereof, which are
reproduced hereunder for ease of reference:
46. Further, in the aforesaid context, we do not think
that there can be any distinction with regard to a piece of
land which has been decided to be constituted as
reserved forest under S. 4(1) of the Act and land being
deemed to be reserved forest under S. 17 of the Act
which is like a final notification. The reason being, once
the land is constituted as reserved forest under S. 4(1) of
the Act, it is by issuance of a Notification then the claims
would have to be made and it is only on the
consideration of the claims that an application for
exclusion of the land constituted as reserved forest under
S. 4(1) of the Act could be ordered. Merely because the
procedure contemplated under the Act subsequent to the
issuance of a Notification under S. 4 of the Act is not yet
completed or no Notification has been issued under S.
17(1) of the Act, in our view, would not make any
difference, as the object and purpose of reserving any
land is to treat the said land as being constituted a
reserved forest. If such a land or any portion thereof is
excluded on adjudication of claims, it would not find a
place in Notification issued under S. 17 of the Act. In
such a case, it would no longer be constituted as
reserved forest. But, till that procedure is not
completed by the Forest Settlement Officer, it
remains to be constituted as reserved forest.
64. Having regard to the definition of forest enunciated
by the Hon'ble Supreme Court, it must be held that all
statutorily recognized forest, whether designated as
reserved forest, protected or otherwise, must be held to
come within the scope of the expression “Forest”. In the
instant case, the land in question was decided to be
constituted as reserved forest under S. 4 of the Act.
Merely because a final declaration under S. 17 of
the Act has not been issued in the instant case, it
cannot be held that subject land is not forest land.
As already noted, when once the land is decided to
be constituted as reserved forest under S. 4 of the

Act, it continues to be forest land until the
procedure contemplated for exclusion of any

reserved forest is completed by adjudication of the
claims. If no claim is made in respect of the land
constituted as reserved forest under S. 4 of the Act, it
fructifies as reserved forest. The object of issuance of
Notification under S. 17 of the Act is to restate the
boundaries of the reserved forest after excluding the
lands constituted as reserved forest on adjudication of
the claims under the Act. Thus, if there are no claims
which are adjudicated, then the land intended to be
constituted as reserved forest is declared as reserved
forest.

49. Most of the well-known dictionaries are more or less
consistent when it comes to the meaning of the word
“forest”. The erstwhile Nagpur High Court in Laxman
Ichharam v. Divl. Forest Officer made an attempt to
define “forests” by referring to dictionary meaning of the
word “forest” in the Oxford English Dictionary. Para 13 of
the said decision reads thus : (SCC OnLine MP)
“13. The term “forest” has not been defined anywhere in
the Forest Act. In the absence of such a definition
the word “forest” must be taken in its ordinary
dictionary sense. The Shorter Oxford English
Dictionary, Vol. I, gives the following meaning to it:
‘1. An extensive tract of land covered with trees and
undergrowth, sometimes intermingled with pasture….
2. Law. A woodland district, usually belonging to the
king, set apart for hunting wild beasts and game, etc.….
3. A wild uncultivated waste.’ ”
65.130. By relying on Narinder Singh, the learned
Additional Advocate General submits that, in
the absence of an exhaustive statutory
definition of the expression “forest”, the
expression must be understood in its ordinary
dictionary sense, having regard to the context
in which it is used. He relies upon the meaning
of the expression referred to in Narinder
Singh, which includes an extensive tract of
land covered with trees and undergrowth,
sometimes intermingled with pasture, a
woodland district, and a wild, uncultivated tract

of land. He contends that the character of a

65.133. He relies on the judgment of the Hon’ble Apex

particularly paragraph 10, which is reproduced

10. According to these sub-sections, a claim for
refund or an order of refund can be made only in
accordance with the provisions of Section 27 which
inter alia includes the period of limitation mentioned
therein. Mr Hidayatullah submitted that the period
of limitation prescribed by Section 27 does not apply
either to a suit filed by the importer or to a writ
petition filed by him and that in such cases the
period of limitation would be three years. The
learned counsel refers to certain decisions of this
Court to that effect. We shall assume for the
purposes of this appeal that it is so, notwithstanding
the fact that the said question is now pending
before a larger Constitution Bench of nine Judges
along with the issue relating to unjust enrichment.
Yet the question is whether it is permissible for the
High Court to direct the authorities under the Act to
act contrary to the aforesaid statutory provision. We

- 237 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
do not think it is, even while acting under Article
226 of the Constitution. The power conferred by
Articles 226/227 is designed to effectuate the
law, to enforce the rule of law and to ensure
that the several authorities and organs of the
State act in accordance with law. It cannot be
invoked for directing the authorities to act
contrary to law. In particular, the Customs
authorities, who are the creatures of the Customs
Act, cannot be directed to ignore or act contrary to
Section 27, whether before or after amendment.
Maybe the High Court or a civil court is not bound
by the said provisions but the authorities under the
Act are. Nor can there be any question of the High
Court clothing the authorities with its power under
Article 226 or the power of a civil court. No such
delegation or conferment can ever be conceived. We
are, therefore, of the opinion that the direction
contained in clause (3) of the impugned order is
unsustainable in law. When we expressed this view
during the hearing Mr Hidayatullah requested that in
such a case the matter be remitted to the High
Court and the High Court be left free to dispose of
the writ petition according to law.
65.134. By relying on Kirloskar Pneumatic, the
Learned Additional Advocate General submits
that Hon’ble Supreme Court has held that the
jurisdiction under Articles 226 and 227 of the
Constitution of India is intended to effectuate
the law, enforce the rule of law and ensure that
State authorities act in accordance with law.
The Hon’ble Supreme Court further observed
that such jurisdiction cannot be invoked to
direct statutory authorities to act contrary to
the provisions of the governing enactment.

65.135. The learned Additional Advocate General
submits that the jurisdiction of this Court under
Article 226 of the Constitution of India cannot
be exercised to compel statutory authorities to
act contrary to the applicable law or to
disregard the statutory restrictions governing
their powers. He contends that, where the
Forest Department authorities are acting in

not to issue directions that would require them

Petitioners cannot seek relief that would have
the effect of restraining the competent
authorities from enforcing the law, merely by
relying upon revenue entries, grants or other
transactions, without establishing that the
rights asserted by them are legally sustainable
under the applicable law.
65.136. He relies on the judgment of the Hon’ble Apex
Court in the case of Jit Ram Shiv Kumar v.
State of Haryana [(1981) 1 SCC 11]
[Paragraph 51], which is reproduced hereunder
for ease of reference:

51. On a consideration of the decisions of this Court
it is clear that there can be no promissory estoppel
against the exercise of legislative power of the
State. So also, the doctrine cannot be invoked for
preventing the Government from acting in
discharge of its duty under the law. The
Government would not be bound by the act of its
officers and agents who act beyond the scope of
their authority and a person dealing with the agent
of the Government must be held to have notice of
the limitations of his authority. the Court can enforce
compliance by a public authority of the obligation laid on
him if he arbitrarily or on his mere whim ignores the
promises made by him on behalf of the Government. It
would be open to the authority to plead and prove that
there were special considerations which necessitated his
not being able to comply with his obligations in public
interest.
65.137. By relying on Jit Ram Shiv Kumar, the
learned Additional Advocate General submits
that the doctrine of promissory estoppel cannot
be invoked to prevent the State from exercising
its legislative powers or discharging its
statutory duties. He contends that the State
cannot be bound by acts or assurances given by
its officers or agents beyond the scope of their
authority and that persons dealing with such
officers are required to take note of the
limitations on their authority. He further
submits that an obligation allegedly arising
from a representation made on behalf of the
Government cannot be enforced where doing so

their existence, entitle them to relief contrary to

65.144. He relies on the judgment of the Hon’ble High
Court of Kerala in the case of CIT v. Punalur
Paper Mills, [(1988) 170 ITR 37], Paragraphs
5 and 6 which are reproduced hereunder for
ease of reference:
5. We see no force in this plea. The Board of
Revenue is competent to issue circulars under
section 119 of the Income-tax Act. The circulars
so issued have got the force of law. All officers
of the Department are bound by the said
circulars. The benevolent circulars issued by the
Board are in the nature of administrative relief.
They really “supplant” the law. The circular can
afford administrative relief even beyond the relevant
terms of the statute. It can deviate from the

- 244 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
provisions of the Act. The courts have held that
such circulars are binding on the officers of the
Department. It is not open to the Department to
contend, even in cases where the circular goes
beyond the terms of the section, that the circular
has no legal effect or should not be given effect to.
The circulars would go to the assistance of the
assessees. It is settled law that the circulars cannot
impose any burden on the taxpayer. But, by the
issue of a circular, the rigour of the law can be
relaxed by giving administrative relief. Apart from
the fact that such circulars are binding on the
officers of the Department, even if the circulars are
relied on for the first time in the High Court during
the course of hearing, the assessee will be entitled
to the benefit afforded by the circular. The court is
bound to take note of the circular. These
propositions are well settled by a series of decisions
of the Supreme Court as well as of High Courts
(Vide Navnit Lal C. Javeri v. K.K. Sen, AAC [1965]
56 ITR 198 at p. 203 (SC), Ellerman Lines Ltd. v.
CIT [1971] 82 ITR 913 (SC), Tala Iron & Steel Co.
Ltd. v. N.C. Upadhyaya [1974] 96 ITR 1 (Bom), F.C.
Agarwal v. CIT [1976] 102 ITR 408 (Gauhati),
Navnitlal Ambalal v. CIT [1976] 105 ITR 735 (Bom),
CWT v. Gammon India P. Ltd. [1981] 130 ITR 471
(Bom) and Addl. CIT v. Mrs. Avtar Mohan Singh
[1982] 136 ITR 645 (Delhi)). This court has also
taken the same view in a series of decisions. They
are: Rajarajeswari Weaving Mills v. ITO [1978] 113
ITR 405, CIT v. B. Al. Edward, India Sea Foods
[1979] 119 ITR 334 [FB] and CIT v. T.S.
Venkiteswaran [1979] 120 ITR 675. It is too late in
the day for the Revenue to contend that the circular
issued by the Board of Revenue is only an
administrative direction or that it will not bind the
Department or that it shall not be given effect to,
since it goes beyond or deviates from the terms of
the statute. As stated, the circular “supplants “ the
law and not “ supplements “ the law.
6. In the light of the above position in law, we are
satisfied that the Appellate Tribunal was justified in
giving effect to the Circular dated

- 245 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024

65.145. By relying on Punalur Paper Mills the Learned
Additional Advocate General submits that
administrative circulars issued by a competent
statutory authority, within the scope of its
powers, have binding effect upon the officers of
the concerned Department. He further contends
that, in the present case, the relevant circulars
and administrative instructions relied upon by
the State must be considered in the context of
their legal authority and applicability to the
forest lands in question.
65.146. He relies on the judgment of the Hon’ble Apex
Court in the case of RBI v. Peerless [(1987) 1
SCC 424], Paragraph 33, which is reproduced
hereunder for ease of reference:
33. Interpretation must depend on the text and the
context. They are the bases of interpretation. One may
well say if the text is the texture, context is what gives
the colour. Neither can be ignored. Both are important.
That interpretation is best which makes the textual
interpretation match the contextual. A statute is best
interpreted when we know why it was enacted. With this
knowledge, the statute must be read, first as a whole and
then section by section, clause by clause, phrase by
phrase and word by word. If a statute is looked at, in the
context of its enactment, with the glasses of the statute-maker, provided by such context, its scheme, the
sections, clauses, phrases and words may take colour
and appear different than when the statute is looked at
without the glasses provided by the context. With these

 HC-KAR

glasses we must look at the Act as a whole and discover
what each section, each clause, each phrase and each
word is meant and designed to say as to fit into the
scheme of the entire Act. No part of a statute and no
word of a statute can be construed in isolation.
Statutes have to be construed so that every word has a
place and everything is in its place. It is by looking at the
definition as a whole in the setting of the entire Act and
by reference to what preceded the enactment and the
reasonsfor it that the Court construed the expression
“Prize Chit” in Srinivasa [(1980) 4 SCC 507 : (1981) 1
SCR 801 : 51 Com Cas 464] and we find no reason to
depart from the Court’s construction.
65.147. By relying on Peerless, the learned Additional
Advocate General submits that the provisions of
the applicable forest legislation must be
interpreted having regard to the text, context,
object and scheme of the enactment as a
whole. He contends that the relevant provisions
cannot be read in isolation or construed solely
on the basis of the meaning attributed to an
individual expression, without considering their
relationship with the other provisions and the
legislative purpose. Accordingly, the legal effect
of the notification dated 05.02.1920 and the
subsequent Government Order dated
11.01.1924 must, according to him, be
determined by examining the relevant
instruments in their proper statutory and

historical context and in the light of the scheme
of the applicable forest legislation.
65.148. On the basis of all the above arguments and
contentions, learned Additional Advocate
General submits that the petition is required to
be dismissed.
66. Heard Sri.Manmohan P.N., learned counsel for the

69. Answer to Point No. (i): Whether the
notification dated 05.02.1920, bearing No.
R.8806-Ft.43-15-6, issued under Section 4 of
the Mysore Forest Regulation, 1900, continues
to remain operative, and whether the
Government Order dated 11.01.1924 had the
effect of withdrawing or cancelling the said
notification or related to a distinct notification
and a limited extent of land?
69.1. Learned counsel for the Petitioners submits that
the proposal to constitute the Moorkangudda
Block as a State Forest, notified on 05.02.1920,
was dropped by the Government of Mysore by
its order dated 11.01.1924. The notification,
therefore, ceased to operate from that date,
and the Forest Department cannot claim
jurisdiction over the Petitioners’ lands on its
basis. He further submits that rights in favour
of the Petitioners were created after the 1920
notification and after the Government’s decision
in 1924; that proceedings founded on the 1920
notification are non est in law; and that, if the
State intends to constitute the lands a reserved
forest, it must initiate fresh proceedings under
the Karnataka Forest Act, 1963. He also relies
on the report dated 11.10.2022 of the Assistant
Commissioner, Sakleshpur Sub-Division, and

the proceedings of the coordination meetings
held under the Regional Commissioner.
69.2. Sri A.M. Vijay submits that a notification once
withdrawn or rendered inoperative cannot be
revived in the absence of statutory authority.
Neither the Mysore Forest Regulation, 1900, nor
the Karnataka Forest Act, 1963, provides for
such revival, particularly after nearly a century
during which the area has become inhabited
and developed. He also relies on the grant of
lands by the State in Kadumane Village from
1952 onwards as conduct consistent with the
Government’s decision of 1924.
69.3. On this basis, the Petitioners in W.P. Nos.
16251/2023, 19980/2023 and 33767/2025
seek a declaration that the 1920 notification
has lapsed or been abandoned. The Petitioner
in W.P. No. 29690/2025 seeks, pursuant to the
amendment allowed on 24.11.2025, a
declaration that the notification is not binding
on its land.
69.4. The learned Additional Advocate General
submits that the Petitioners’ lands fall within
the Moorkangudda Block, stated to comprise

69.6. The issue turns on the Government records and
the legal effect of the orders recorded therein.
Both sides accept that the notification dated
05.02.1920 was published, that the
Government passed an order on 11.01.1924,
and that a notification of that date was
published in the Gazette. The dispute concerns
the decision taken by the Government in 1924.
That question must be answered from the

contemporaneous documents relied upon by the

69.7. The relevant provisions of the Mysore Forest
Regulation, 1900, are Sections 4 and 17.
Section 4 provides:
4. Whenever it is proposed to constitute any
land a State Forest, the Government shall
publish a notification in the official Gazette—
(a) specifying as nearly as possible the situation and
limits of such land; (b) declaring that it is proposed
to constitute such land a State Forest; and (c)
appointing an officer (hereinafter called ‘the Forest
Settlement Officer’) to inquire into and determine
the existence, nature and extent of any rights
claimed by, or alleged to exist in favour of any
person in or over any land comprised within such
limits, or to any forest produce of such land, and to
deal with the same as provided in this chapter.
69.8. Section 4 provides for publication of a proposal
to constitute land a State Forest and for inquiry
into the rights claimed in respect of that land.
The notification issued under that provision
does not, by itself, constitute the land a State
Forest. That status is conferred by the
notification contemplated under Section 17.
After the claims and appeals have been
disposed of, the proceedings required by the
Regulation have been completed and the lands
to be acquired have vested in the Government,

- 256 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
Section 17 provides for publication of a
notification specifying the limits of the forest
and declaring it to be a State Forest from a
date fixed therein. Under Section 17(2), the
forest is deemed to be a State Forest from that
date.
69.9. The Government is not bound to complete the
process merely because it has issued a
notification under Section 4. It may decide not
to proceed with the proposal. The notification
under Section 4 initiates the statutory process;
it does not compel the Government to carry
that process to its conclusion.
69.10. The notification No. R.8806-Ft.43-15-6, dated
05.02.1920, published on 12.02.1920, declares
that it is proposed to constitute the lands
described in its Schedule as a State Forest. The
Schedule identifies the Moorkangudda Block in
Manjarabad Taluk, comprising Achanahalli,
Agini, Kyamanahalli, Nadahalli, Kadumane,
Jambardi, Manjanahalli Estate and Kadumane
Estate, with an extent of about 7,938 acres 38
guntas. It also appoints the Forest Settlement
Officer and the appellate authority.

69.11. The separate notification dated 05.02.1920,
bearing No. R.8808-Ft.43-15-7, was issued
under Section 6 of the Mysore Land Acquisition
Regulation, VII of 1894, in respect of private
holdings aggregating 68 acres and 33 guntas,
required for the reservation of the
Moorkangudda Forest Block. It was published in
the Mysore Gazette dated 12.02.1920. It was
distinct from the preliminary notification dated
05.02.1920, bearing No. R.8806-Ft.43-15-6,
issued under Section 4 of the Mysore Forest
Regulation, XI of 1900. The former initiated
acquisition of the specified private holdings; it
did not itself propose to constitute the entire
Moorkangudda Block as a State Forest.
69.12. The file of the General and Revenue Secretariat,

are on record, discloses the subsequent course
of events. By letter No. 712, received on
29.04.1923, the Conservator of Forests wrote:
“Adverting to G.O. No.R.8803-5/Ft.43-15-5
dated the 5th February 1920, sanctioning the
reservation of the Moorkangudda Forest Block
in the Manjerabad Taluk and the issue of the
notification under Section 4 of the Forest
Regulation, I have the honour to state that
during my recent tour in the Hassan District, I

69.13. The Secretariat thereafter sought information
as to whether any land had been acquired and

preliminary demarcation line had been cut and
that “no compensation was paid as no lands
were acquired”. The Secretariat then recorded
that “the reservation should be cancelled”.
69.14. By a further letter No.595 dated 17.12.1923,
the Conservator forwarded the settlement
records and reiterated that the area comprised
treeless grassy plains and that the proposal
could be dropped.

“Under Section 48 (1) of the Mysore Land

69.20. The typed copy of the Government proceedings
recording Entry 272 refers to the Mysore Land
Acquisition Regulation, VII of 1891. However,
the separate acquisition notification No.
R.8808-Ft.43-15-7 dated 05.02.1920 identifies
the applicable Regulation as VII of 1894. The
Gazette notification dated 11.01.1924,
published on 17.01.1924 to withdraw that
acquisition, also refers to the Mysore Land
Acquisition Regulation, VII of 1894. The
discrepancy in the reference to the Regulation
in the typed copy of Entry 272 is noted. The
statutory references in the two Gazette
notifications are reproduced as they appear in
those documents.
69.21. For clarity, the records disclose two distinct
notifications dated 05.02.1920 and two
separate Government actions dated
11.01.1924. Notification No. R.8806-Ft.43-15-6
was the preliminary notification issued under
Section 4 of the Mysore Forest Regulation, XI of
1900, proposing to constitute the
Moorkangudda Block, measuring approximately
7,938 acres and 38 guntas, as a State Forest.
Notification No. R.8808-Ft.43-15-7 was a

separate notification issued under Section 6 of
the Mysore Land Acquisition Regulation, VII of
1894, for acquisition of 68 acres and 33 guntas
of private lands required for that purpose. On
11.01.1924, Entry 271, bearing No. I.C.
4236.8-Ft.296-22-6, recorded the
Government's decision to drop the proposal to
constitute the Moorkangudda Block as a State
Forest. Entry 272, bearing No. I.C. 4239-
Ft.296-22-7, recorded the consequential
withdrawal, under Section 48(1) of the Land
Acquisition Regulation of the acquisition of the
68 acres and 33 guntas. The latter notification
was published in the Mysore Gazette dated
17.01.1924. Thus, Entry 271 concerns the
Section 4 proposal for the entire Moorkangudda
Block, whereas Entry 272 concerns only the
separate acquisition proceedings relating to 68
acres and 33 guntas.
69.22. These documents resolve the first part of the
dispute. On 11.01.1924, the Government made
two distinct decisions, recorded separately as
IC 4236.8 and IC 4239. By the first, it dropped
the proposal to constitute the Moorkangudda
Block a State Forest. By the second, it withdrew

the acquisition of the 68 acres 33 guntas
required for that purpose.
69.23. The withdrawal of acquisition was consequential
upon the decision to abandon the proposal to
constitute the forest. The acquisition
notification itself states that the properties were
required for the constitution of the
Moorkangudda State Forest. Once the
Government decided not to constitute the
forest, the acquisition for that purpose was no
longer required. Paragraph 2 of the order
expressly provides that a notification
withdrawing the acquisition “will be published”.
It follows the decision recorded in paragraph 1;
it does not replace or limit that decision.
69.24. The learned Additional Advocate General
submits that the Government Order concerned
only the separate acquisition notification No.
R.8808-Ft.43-15-7 and the limited extent of 68
acres 33 guntas. That submission reads the
consequential withdrawal of acquisition in
isolation and fails to account for the principal
decision recorded as item 271 (IC 4236.8). It
cannot be accepted for the following reasons.

- 264 -

69.24.1. The order expressly directs that “the
proposal to constitute the area into a
State Forest be dropped”. Section 4 of
the Regulation provides for publication of
precisely such a proposal. The only
notification proposing to constitute the
Moorkangudda Block a State Forest was
the notification dated 05.02.1920,
bearing No. R.8806-Ft.43-15-6. A
notification under the Land Acquisition
Regulation declares that land is required
for a public purpose; it does not propose
to constitute a forest. No other
instrument answers the description used
in the Government order.
69.24.2. The Government’s own record confirms
this distinction. The Proceedings list the
1920 notification under the Forest
Regulation as the first matter and the
acquisition notification as the second.
The order then deals with “the proposal
to constitute the area” in paragraph 1
and “the properties referred to above” in
paragraph 2. The two paragraphs

- 265 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
correspond to the two matters listed in
the Proceedings.
69.24.3. The Government accepted the
Conservator’s recommendation that the
area comprised treeless grassy plains
and that conserving the Block would be
to the detriment of the villagers. That
reason concerned the Block as a whole,
not merely the 68 acres 33 guntas of
private holdings. The Conservator had
recommended that further proceedings
be dropped and that the Section 4
notification be “treated as cancelled”.
The Secretariat recorded that “the
reservation should be cancelled”, and the
file itself was titled “Cancelling the
reservation”. The decision cannot
reasonably be read as applying only to
the limited extent proposed for
acquisition.
69.24.4. The learned Additional Advocate
General’s own submission supports this
conclusion. He states that, when the
1920 notification was issued, all lands

- 266 -
 HC-KAR

AND 3 OTHERS
within the Block, except 68 acres 33
guntas, were already at the disposal of
the State Government, and that only the
latter extent required acquisition. Thus,
68 acres 33 guntas was the extent
sought to be acquired, not the extent
covered by the proposal to constitute a
State Forest. The proposal covered the
entire Block. When the Government
dropped “the proposal”, it dropped the
proposal for the entire Block. The limited
extent relied upon by the Respondents
pertains to the consequential withdrawal
of acquisition, not to the principal
decision.
69.24.5. If the Government had intended to deal
only with acquisition, there would have
been no reason to direct that the
proposal to constitute the area a State
Forest be dropped, to communicate that
decision to the Conservator of Forests,
the Revenue Commissioner and the
Deputy Commissioner, or to record it in
the digest under the heading
“Moorkangudda Forest Block”.

- 267 -
 HC-KAR

69.25. The learned Additional Advocate General also
relies on RBI v. Peerless [(1987) 1 SCC 424],
paragraph 33, to submit that the 1920
notification and the 1924 order must be read in
their statutory and historical context. The
principle that text must be read in context, and
that no part of a statute or word may be
construed in isolation, is sound. The same
discipline applies when construing a
Government order. Here, the text, context and
purpose of the order establish that the proposal
to constitute the entire Block a State Forest was
dropped.
69.26. The next question is whether the Government’s
decision was effective in the absence of a
separate notification under the Regulation
withdrawing the Section 4 notification. It was.
69.27. The Regulation prescribes no particular form for
the Government to abandon a proposal notified
under Section 4. Nor has any party identified a
provision requiring a further notification for that
purpose. Section 17 confers a power on the
Government to issue a final notification; it does
not compel the Government to complete the

process once a proposal has been notified. A
decision not to proceed with the proposal,
therefore, did not require a notification in the
form prescribed for constituting a State Forest.
69.28. The Government recorded its decision in the
Secretariat file and communicated it to the
officers responsible for carrying the process
forward. It also published the notification
required to withdraw the acquisition. The order
was a decision of the Government, made and
recorded in the manner reflected in its official
proceedings. The absence of a separate
notification withdrawing the Section 4
notification does not nullify that decision.
69.29. The Government’s subsequent conduct is
consistent with the decision of 1924. The
Respondents have produced no proclamation,
inquiry or final notification concerning the Block
after January 1924 before 2022.
69.30. The Revenue Department auctioned land in
Achanahalli Village on 20.09.1924, eight
months after the Government’s decision. The
Petitioners state that the Government granted

69.31. No notification concerning the Block under the
Karnataka Forest Act, 1963, which came into

According to the learned Additional Advocate

appointed by Government Order dated

69.32. The suggestion that the auction in 1924 was

claim is unsupported by any document before
this Court. It also fails to explain why the
Government had itself directed, eight months
earlier, that the proposal to constitute the Block
a State Forest be dropped.

69.34. The 1920 notification remains part of the

69.35. The contention that the Petitioners have taken

69.36. This Court answers Point No. (i) by holding that
the notification dated 05.02.1920, bearing No.
R.8806-Ft.43-15-6, does not remain operative
as a proposal to constitute the Moorkangudda
Block a State Forest. By the Government
decision recorded in Entry 271, bearing No. I.C.
4236.8-Ft.296-22-6, dated 11.01.1924, the
Government dropped the proposal for the entire
Block. The decision was not confined to the 68
acres and 33 guntas covered by the separate
acquisition notification. Entry 272, bearing No.
I.C. 4239-Ft.296-22-7, dated 11.01.1924, and
published in the Mysore Gazette dated
17.01.1924, records the consequential
withdrawal of acquisition under Notification No.
R.8808-Ft.43-15-7. The Respondents' contrary
contention is rejected.
70. Answer to Point No. (ii): What is the legal
effect of the absence of a final notification
under Section 17 of the Mysore Forest
Regulation, 1900, and whether the absence of
such notification precludes the State from
asserting the forest status of the notified lands
or taking action under the applicable forest
legislation?

70.1. Sri Manmohan P.N., learned counsel for the
Petitioners, submits that, even assuming that
the notification dated 05.02.1920 had remained
in force, it could not, by itself, authorise the
Forest Department to dispossess the
Petitioners. Unless the proceedings under the
Mysore Forest Regulation, 1900 were completed
and a final notification under Section 17 was
issued, the Respondents could not treat the
lands as a reserved forest or invoke Section
64A of the Karnataka Forest Act, 1963 on that
basis. He submits that the expression “deemed
forest” cannot furnish an independent ground
for dispossession and that the Respondents
must first establish the legal status of the
lands.
70.2. Sri Manmohan P.N. further submits that Section
17 of the Regulation prescribes the stages
which must be completed before a final
notification can be issued. The period for
preferring claims must have expired and the
claims must have been disposed of; the period
for preferring appeals must have expired and all

including the acquisition of lands and buildings
selected for that purpose, must have been
completed. None of these stages was
completed in respect of Moorkangudda Block.
No final notification under Section 17 was
issued. The lands were therefore never
constituted a State Forest under the Regulation.
He also relies on the report of the Assistant
Commissioner dated 11.10.2022 in support of
the Petitioners’ case.
70.3. Sri Manmohan P.N. relies on M/s. Bharat
Parikh & Co. v. State of Karnataka [W.P. No.
22514/2018], decided on 18.06.2019,
particularly paragraphs 5 to 7. A Coordinate
Bench held that a notification under Section
4(1) of the 1963 Act is only the first step in the
process of constituting a reserved forest and
that the land acquires that status only upon
publication of a notification under Section 17.
The Bench expressly left open the question
whether the land constituted forest in the wider
sense under the Forest (Conservation) Act,
1980.

70.4. Sri A.M. Vijay, learned counsel for the
Petitioners in W.P. No. 29690/2025, adopts the
submissions of Sri Manmohan P.N. on this point.
70.5. Sri Kiran Ron, learned Additional Advocate
General, does not contend that a final
notification under Section 17 of the Regulation
was issued in respect of Moorkangudda Block.
His submission is that the notification dated
05.02.1920 was not a mere expression of
intention. It attracted statutory restrictions on
the acquisition of rights in the notified area,
and its legal effect must be examined under the
Regulation rather than determined solely by
reference to subsequent revenue entries, grants
and transactions. He submits that the
Regulation prescribes no outer time limit for
publication of the final notification and that the
absence of such notification does not, by itself,
determine the legal consequences of the
preliminary notification.
70.6. Sri Kiran Ron relies on B.R. Ganapathi Singh
v. State of Karnataka [2020 SCC OnLine Kar
3074], particularly paragraphs 46 and 64. He
submits that land covered by a subsisting

are adjudicated and the land, where

Kar 10537], particularly paragraphs 8, 10 to 17
and 19. According to him, these decisions
establish that the absence of a final notification
does not, by itself, extinguish the statutory
restrictions attached to a preliminary
notification.
70.7. Sri Kiran Ron also relies on Narinder Singh v.
Divesh Bhutani [2022 SCC OnLine SC 899],
particularly paragraphs 41 and 49. He submits
that, in the absence of a statutory definition,
the expression “forest” bears its ordinary
dictionary meaning and that land recorded as
forest in Government records attracts Section 2
of the Forest (Conservation) Act, 1980. On that
basis, he contends that the shola forests and
grasslands of Moorkangudda constitute forest

particularly paragraph 4, to contend that
revenue entries and saguvali chits confer no

70.8. This point raises two distinct questions. The
first is whether the absence of a final
notification under Section 17 prevented
Moorkangudda Block from acquiring the status
of a State Forest or reserved forest. The second
is whether, notwithstanding the absence of such
notification, the State can rely on the
preliminary notification dated 05.02.1920 to

70.9. Section 17 of the Regulation prescribes the
stages which must precede publication of the
final notification. The period fixed under Section
5 for preferring claims must have expired and
the claims must have been disposed of by the
Forest Settlement Officer. Where claims have
been made, the period for preferring appeals
under Section 15 must have expired and the
appeals must have been disposed of. The
proceedings prescribed by Sections 10 and 13
must also have been completed, including the
vesting in the Government of lands and
buildings which the Forest Settlement Officer
elected to acquire under the Land Acquisition
Regulation, 1894.
70.10. Upon completion of those stages, Section
17(1)(d) provides that the Government may
publish a notification specifying the limits of the
forest and declaring it to be a State Forest from
the date fixed in that notification. Section 17(2)
provides that, from that date, the forest shall
be deemed to be a State Forest. The final

- 278 -

AND 3 OTHERS
notification is thus the statutory instrument by
which the proposed forest acquires the status of
a State Forest under the Regulation.
70.11. No notification under Section 17 has been
identified or produced in respect of
Moorkangudda Block. The Respondents do not
dispute its absence. The lands were therefore
never constituted a State Forest under the
Regulation.
70.12. The same conclusion follows under the 1963
Act. Section 2(14) defines a “Reserved Forest”
as land settled and notified as such in
accordance with Chapter II of that Act. Section
23(1) recognises as reserved forests under the
1963 Act forests which had already been
notified as State Forests under the Mysore
Forest Regulations, 1900, before the 1963 Act
came into force. It does not confer the status of
a reserved forest on land which was merely the
subject of a preliminary notification under
Section 4 of the Regulation and was never
declared a State Forest under Section 17. Since
Moorkangudda Block was never constituted a
State Forest, Section 23 does not apply to it.

Section 23 is reproduced hereunder for ease of
reference:
23. Reserved forests constituted previous to
passing of this Act.— (1) Any forest which has
been notified as a State Forest under the Mysore
Forest Act, 1900, or as a reserved forest under the
Indian Forest Act, 1927, the Madras Forest Act,
1882, or the Hyderabad Forest Act, 1355F, prior to
the date on which this Act comes into force, shall be
a reserved forest under this Act:
Provided that if the rights of private persons to or
over any land or forest produce in such forest shall
not have been inquired into, settled and recorded in
a manner which the State Government deems
sufficient, the same shall be inquired into, settled
and recorded in the manner provided by this Act;
and until such inquiry, settlement and record have
been completed, the operation of this section shall
not abridge, or affect such rights.
(2) All questions decided, orders issued and records
prepared in connection with the constitution of such
forest as a State Forest or reserved forest shall be
deemed to have been decided, issued and prepared
under this Act, and the provisions of this Act
relating to reserved forests shall apply to forests to
which the provisions of sub-section (1) are
applicable.
70.13. The decision in M/s. Bharat Parikh & Co.
supports this distinction. The Coordinate Bench
held that a notification under Section 4(1) of
the 1963 Act is “only a first step which is
required to be taken before the State
Government decides to constitute a land as a
reserved forest” and that the land is declared a

- 280 -
 HC-KAR

reserved forest only after publication of a
notification under Section 17. Though that
decision concerned the 1963 Act, the distinction
between the preliminary notification and the
final declaration applies equally to the scheme
of the Regulation. Section 4 initiates the
process; Section 17 completes it and confers
the status of a State Forest. The Coordinate
Bench also expressly left open the question
whether the land constituted forest land within
the meaning of the 1980 Act. This Court adopts
the same distinction.
70.14. The absence of a final notification, however,
does not by itself answer the second question.
A preliminary notification may attract statutory
consequences before the land acquires the final
status of a State Forest or reserved forest. The
nature, commencement and duration of those
consequences must be determined from the
applicable statutory provisions. They cannot be
equated with the legal status conferred by a
final notification under Section 17.
70.15. Section 6 of the Regulation is material. Section
6(1) prohibits the acquisition of rights in or over

- 281 -

the notified land, subject to the exceptions
specified in that provision. It also restricts the
construction of new houses, formation of
plantations, fresh clearings and cutting of trees
for trade or manufacture. It further prohibits
the grant of a right of occupancy without the
previous sanction of the Government. The
prohibition under Section 6(1), however,
operates during the interval between
publication of the proclamation contemplated
by Section 5 and the date fixed by the
notification declaring the forest to be a State
Forest. It does not commence merely upon
publication of the notification under Section 4.
Section 6(2) preserves acts done with the
written permission of the Forest Settlement
Officer.
70.16. It follows that the prohibition under Section
6(1) cannot be applied to every transaction
entered into after the notification dated
05.02.1920 merely on the basis of that
notification. The statutory trigger prescribed by
Section 6(1) must first be established.

- 282 -

70.17. Section 6(3) deals with a different matter. It
bars a Civil Court, during the period between
publication of the notification under Section 4
and the final notification under Section 17, from
entertaining a suit to establish a right in or over
the notified land or its forest produce. It does
not prohibit the acquisition of rights in the same
terms as Section 6(1), nor does it confer on the
Forest Department a power of eviction. It is a
procedural bar ancillary to the statutory process
for constituting a State Forest. Once the
Government formally dropped that proposal,
the provision could not continue to operate as
though the process remained pending. It
cannot be used to preserve indefinitely the
consequences of a proposal which the
Government itself had abandoned.
70.18. In Prabhagiya Van Adhikari Awadh Van
Prabhag v. Arun Kumar Bhardwaj, the
Hon’ble Supreme Court held that, even in the
absence of a final notification, a statutory bar
on the acquisition of rights could continue to
operate after publication of a preliminary
notification. The decision must be read in the
context of the statutory provision considered by

- 283 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
the Supreme Court. It concerned Section 5 of
the Indian Forest Act, 1927, as substituted by
the Uttar Pradesh Act 23 of 1965. That
substituted provision expressly prohibited the
acquisition of rights in land after publication of
a notification under Section 4, subject to the
exceptions specified in it.
70.19. The decision establishes that a preliminary
notification may attract an express statutory
bar on the acquisition of rights. It does not
permit the Court to disregard the wording of
the corresponding provision in the Regulation.
Section 6(1) of the Regulation ties the
prohibition on the acquisition of rights to
publication of the proclamation under Section 5.
The statutory trigger under the Regulation is
therefore different from the one considered in
Arun Kumar Bhardwaj. The decision cannot
be applied without regard to that distinction.
Further, the statutory restrictions recognised in
that decision presuppose a subsisting
notification. It does not hold that those
restrictions continue after the Government has
withdrawn the proposal on which they depend.

70.20. In B.R. Ganapathi Singh, a co-ordinate bench
of this Court considered a notification issued
under Section 4(1) of the 1963 Act. The co-ordinate bench held that, notwithstanding the
absence of a final notification under Section 17,
land covered by a subsisting Section 4(1)
notification continued to be forest land for the
purposes of the 1980 Act until the claims were
adjudicated and the boundaries of the proposed
forest were settled. The decision recognises
that the absence of a final notification does not,
by itself, remove the consequences of a
preliminary notification which remains in force.
70.21. That proposition does not assist the
Respondents in the present case. The decision
concerned a subsisting notification under
Section 4(1) of the 1963 Act. This Court has
already held under point (i) that the
Government Order dated 11.01.1924 (IC
4236.8) dropped the proposal contained in the
notification dated 05.02.1920 for the entire
Moorkangudda Block. Notification IC 4239 was
the consequential withdrawal of the acquisition
proceedings relating to 68 acres 33 guntas. The
notification of 1920 did not remain in force as a

live proposal after 11.01.1924. B.R. Ganapathi
Singh does not hold that the consequences of a
preliminary notification survive the
Government’s withdrawal of the proposal itself.
70.22. Girish H.E. also concerns the statutory process
under the 1963 Act. The observations in
paragraph 19 recognise the restrictions which
follow a notification under Section 4 of that Act,
including the restrictions under Section 6.
Those observations apply where the notification
under Section 4 remains in force. They do not
concern a proposal under the Regulation which
the Government subsequently withdrew. The
decision therefore does not establish that the
restrictions under the 1963 Act can be founded
on a notification which had ceased to operate
before the 1963 Act came into force.
70.23. These decisions lay down principles which
operate in their respective statutory settings.
M/s. Bharat Parikh & Co. explains that a
preliminary notification does not, by itself,
confer the final status of a reserved forest. B.R.
Ganapathi Singh recognises that a subsisting
notification under Section 4(1) of the 1963 Act

- 286 -

AND 3 OTHERS
may have consequences under the 1980 Act
even before publication of the final notification.
Arun Kumar Bhardwaj applies an express
statutory bar contained in the Uttar Pradesh
amendment to the Indian Forest Act, 1927.
Girish H.E. concerns the restrictions under the
1963 Act. None of these decisions holds that
statutory restrictions continue after the
Government has withdrawn the proposal on
which they rest. There is no conflict between
these authorities.
70.24. The Petitioners’ submission concerning “deemed
forest” requires a further distinction. Section
17(2) of the Regulation deems land to be a
State Forest from the date fixed in the final
notification. That provision was never attracted
in respect of Moorkangudda Block. The lands
were therefore never deemed to be a State
Forest under the Regulation.
70.25. That conclusion does not determine whether
any parcel is forest land under the 1980 Act or
under another applicable provision of law. The
expression “forest land” under the 1980 Act is
not confined to land formally declared a

- 287 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024

WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
reserved forest. The question whether any
parcel falls within that wider meaning must be
examined independently.
70.26. In Narinder Singh v. Divesh Bhutani, the
Hon’ble Supreme Court considered the meaning
of “forest” for the purposes of Section 2 of the
1980 Act. It held that, in the absence of a
statutory definition, the word “forest” bears its
ordinary dictionary meaning. It also recognised
that land shown as forest in Government
records attracts Section 2 of the 1980 Act,
provided the relevant entries were made after
due process. This Court applies those
principles.
70.27. Those principles do not, however, create a
power of summary eviction under Section 64A
of the 1963 Act or confer jurisdiction on a
Forest Settlement Officer under Section 5 and
the succeeding provisions of that Act. The
exercise of those powers must satisfy the
conditions prescribed by the 1963 Act. The
existence of forest land under the 1980 Act and
the availability of a particular statutory power
under the 1963 Act are separate questions.

- 288 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
