WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
70.28. Whether a particular parcel is forest in the
ordinary dictionary sense or is recorded as
forest in a Government record prepared after
due process, is a question of fact. It requires
examination of the nature of the parcel, the
relevant records, the manner in which those
records came into existence, and the identity
and boundaries of the land. Sri Kiran Ron
submits that the character of the area must be
assessed with reference to the forest records,
the notification and its ecological features,
rather than solely by reference to the present
physical appearance of individual parcels.
70.29. The general reference to forest entries in
Government records does not establish that
each Petitioner's property is forest land. The
relevant records must be identified, their
provenance and legal basis examined, and the
entries matched to the particular parcels
claimed by the Petitioners. The present record
does not permit a parcel-wise finding on
whether any land in Moorkangudda Block is
forest in the ordinary dictionary sense. Nor
does the description of the area in the 1924
documents as treeless grassy plains conclude

- 289 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
that question. The ordinary meaning of forest
may include extensive tracts in which pasture
and woodland occur together. The character of
each parcel must therefore be determined on
the relevant evidence and under the applicable
law.
70.30. I.S. Nirvane Gowda concerned land which had
been included in a reserved forest. The Hon’ble
Supreme Court held that revenue entries were
of no consequence in relation to such land and
that the Revenue Authorities could not deal
with it. The decision applies where the legal
status of the land as a reserved forest has first
been established. It does not establish that land
which was never constituted a reserved forest
under the applicable statute must be treated as
one.
70.31. Sri Kiran Ron also relies on the circular dated
20.08.2004 and the order of the Hon’ble
Supreme Court dated 13.11.2000 in Centre for
Environmental Law, WWF-India v. Union of
India. He submits that an area covered by a
notification under Section 4 cannot be deleted

- 290 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
without the permission of the Hon’ble Supreme
Court.
70.32. That procedure has no application to the finding
under point (i). The Government Order dated
11.01.1924 dropped the proposal to constitute
the entire Moorkangudda Block as a State
Forest. Recognising the effect of that order is
not an act of deletion or dereservation of an
area from a subsisting forest notification. This
judgment does not direct the deletion of any
area from a notification which remains in force,
nor does it authorise non-forest use of land
which is otherwise established to be forest land.
If any parcel in Moorkangudda Block is forest
land within the meaning of the 1980 Act, the
restrictions imposed by that Act continue to
apply.
70.33. Sri Kiran Ron also relies on CIT v. Punalur
Paper Mills [(1988) 170 ITR 37], particularly
paragraphs 5 and 6, for the proposition that
circulars issued by a competent authority within
its statutory powers bind the officers of the
department. The Hon’ble Kerala High Court
recognised the binding effect of circulars issued

by the competent authority under the Income-tax Act. The principle is accepted. It does not
advance the Respondents’ case. A departmental
circular may regulate the manner in which
officers exercise an existing power. It cannot
create a statutory notification, revive a proposal
already withdrawn, or confer jurisdiction which
the statute does not provide. The circular dated
20.08.2004 cannot, therefore, revive the
proposal withdrawn in 1924.
70.34. The legal position follows from the statutory
scheme and the finding under point (i). The
absence of a final notification under Section 17
means that Moorkangudda Block was never
constituted a State Forest under the Regulation.
It did not acquire the status of a reserved forest
under Section 2(14) of the 1963 Act, nor does
it fall within Section 23 of that Act as a forest
previously notified as a State Forest.
70.35. A preliminary notification may attract statutory
restrictions before the final notification is
issued. The restrictions under Section 6 of the
Regulation, however, operate in accordance
with the conditions and time periods specified

- 292 -

in that provision. They cannot be detached from
the statutory process and continued after the
Government has withdrawn the proposal. The
Government Order dated 11.01.1924 (IC
4236.8) dropped the proposal to constitute the
entire Block as a State Forest. Notification IC
4239 was consequential and withdrew the
acquisition proceedings relating to 68 acres 33
guntas. The Respondents cannot rely on the
1920 notification as a continuing source of
restrictions or as the basis for proceedings
against the Petitioners.
70.36. The repeal-and-savings provision in Section 117
of the 1963 Act cannot revive a notification or
proposal which the Government had withdrawn
before the 1963 Act came into force. The
finding under point (i), that the proposal had
been dropped in 1924, governs that
consideration.
70.37. These findings do not determine whether any
particular parcel is forest land under the 1980
Act or falls within another category recognised
by the 1963 Act. The State may establish such
status on an independent legal and factual

basis and take action authorised by the
applicable law. The existence of forest land
under the 1980 Act does not, by itself, confer
jurisdiction to invoke Section 64A or to conduct
proceedings before a Forest Settlement Officer.
Each statutory power must be exercised only
after establishing the conditions which confer it.
Since the proposal under the 1920 notification
was withdrawn, the State cannot use that
notification as a substitute for a fresh decision
under Section 4(1) of the 1963 Act if it seeks to
constitute the lands as a reserved forest.
70.38. This Court therefore answers Point No. (ii) by
holding that no final notification under Section
17 of the Mysore Forest Regulation, 1900, was
issued in respect of Moorkangudda Block. The
lands were never constituted a State Forest
under the Regulation and did not acquire the
status of a reserved forest under Section 2(14)
of the Karnataka Forest Act, 1963, or under
Section 23 of that Act.
70.39. The absence of a final notification does not, by
itself, extinguish statutory restrictions attached
to a subsisting preliminary notification. In the

present case, however, the Government Order
dated 11.01.1924 (IC 4236.8) dropped the

Notification IC 4239 was consequential and
related to the withdrawal of acquisition
proceedings for 68 acres 33 guntas. The
Respondents cannot rely on the withdrawn
proposal as a continuing basis to assert that the
lands constitute a State Forest or reserved
forest, or to initiate proceedings against the
Petitioners.
71. Answer to Point No. (iii): Whether the repeal of
the Mysore Forest Regulation, 1900, by the
Karnataka Forest Act, 1963, affects the validity
or continued operation of the notification dated
05.02.1920 and the proceedings initiated
pursuant thereto, having regard to the
applicable repeal-and-savings provisions?
71.1. Sri Manmohan P.N., learned counsel for the
Petitioners, submits that, after the repeal of the
Regulation by the Karnataka Forest Act, 1963,
every action of the Forest Department must
derive its authority from the 1963 Act and

comply with the procedure prescribed therein.
He submits that a notification under Section 4
of the Mysore Forest Regulation, 1900 is
fundamentally different from a notification
under Section 4(1) of the 1963 Act. The former
announces a proposal to constitute land as a
State Forest, whereas the latter declares that
the State Government has decided to constitute
land as a reserved forest. The subsequent
proceedings, including the appointment of the
Forest Settlement Officer, publication of the
proclamation and inquiry into claims, must
therefore satisfy the requirements of the 1963
Act. Even if the notification dated 05.02.1920
had survived, it remained a proposal under the
Regulation and could not, by itself, be treated
as a decision under Section 4(1) of the 1963
Act. A proclamation under Section 5 of the
1963 Act could not be issued without a
notification satisfying Section 4(1).
71.2. Sri A.M. Vijay, learned counsel for the
Petitioners in W.P. No. 29690/2025, advances
the same distinction between a proposal under
the Regulation and a decision under the 1963
Act. He submits that the issue of a

71.3. Sri Manmohan P.N. relies on M. Karunanidhi
v. Union of India [(1979) 3 SCC 431],
particularly paragraph 35, and Union of India
v. Rajeev Bansal [2024 SCC OnLine SC 2693],
particularly paragraphs 42 and 43. He submits
that repeal by implication is not readily
inferred. It arises only where the provisions of
the two enactments are so inconsistent that
they cannot operate together. The scope, object
and scheme of both enactments must be
examined before such a conclusion is reached.
On that basis, he contends that the
Respondents cannot rely on the 1963 Act to
dispense with the statutory process under the
Regulation or avoid the requirement of a final
notification under Section 17. They must
establish the legal basis on which the lands
acquired the status of a reserved forest.
71.4. Sri Kiran Ron, learned Additional Advocate
General, submits that Section 117 of the 1963
Act expressly repealed the earlier forest

Point (ii) has further held that the lands were

(b) any right, privilege, obligation or liability

(c) any penalty, forfeiture or punishment incurred in

(d) any investigation, legal proceeding or remedy in

liability, penalty, forfeiture or punishment as
aforesaid; and any such investigation, legal
proceeding or remedy, may be instituted, continued
or enforced, and any such penalty, forfeiture or
punishment may be imposed as if this Act had not
been passed:
Provided further that, subject to the preceding
proviso, anything done or any action taken
(including any appointment or delegation made,
notification, order, instruction or direction issued,
rule, regulation, form, or scheme framed, certificate
obtained, permit or licence granted or registration
effected) under any such law shall be deemed to
have been done or taken under the corresponding
provision of this Act and shall continue to be in force
accordingly, unless and until superseded by
anything done or any action taken under this Act.
71.8. Section 117 of the 1963 Act is the repeal-and-savings provision. It expressly repeals, among
other enactments, the Mysore Forest
Regulations, 1900, as in force in the Mysore

Area. The first proviso preserves the previous
operation of the repealed law, anything duly
done or suffered under it, rights, privileges,
obligations and liabilities acquired, accrued or
incurred under it, and investigations, legal
proceedings and remedies relating to those
matters. It further provides that such
investigations, proceedings and remedies may
be instituted, continued or enforced as if the
1963 Act had not been passed.
71.9. The second proviso reads:
“Provided further that, subject to the preceding
proviso, anything done or any action taken
(including any appointment or delegation made,
notification, order, instruction or direction issued,
rule, regulation, form, or scheme framed, certificate
obtained, permit or licence granted or registration
effected) under any such law shall be deemed to
have been done or taken under the corresponding
provision of this Act and shall continue to be in force
accordingly, unless and until superseded by
anything done or any action taken under this Act.”
71.10. The two provisos serve distinct purposes. The
first preserves the past operation of the
repealed law and the rights, obligations,
liabilities and proceedings which accrued under
it. The second gives continuity under the
corresponding provisions of the new Act to

- 301 -
 HC-KAR

actions and instruments taken or issued under
the repealed law. Neither proviso can be read in
isolation from the other or beyond the terms
used by the Legislature.
71.11. Section 117 does not extinguish what was
validly done under the repealed enactment. It
preserves the legal consequences which had
accrued under that enactment. It also continues
actions and instruments which remained in
force when the 1963 Act came into operation,
by treating them as actions taken under the
corresponding provisions of the new Act. The
words “shall continue to be in force accordingly”
are decisive. They preserve continuity; they do
not revive an action which had already ceased
to operate before the new Act commenced.
71.12. The notification dated 05.02.1920 must be
examined on that basis. The 1963 Act came
into force on 01.06.1969. The proposal
contained in the 1920 notification had been
dropped by the Government order dated
11.01.1924. The notification was therefore not
a subsisting proposal when Section 117 came
into operation. The second proviso cannot

revive it. This conclusion follows from the text
of Section 117 and the finding already reached
under point (i). The position may be stated as
under:
71.12.1. The repeal was express. Section 117
specifically names the Mysore Forest Act,
1900, as in force in the Mysore Area,
among the enactments repealed by the
1963 Act. The principles governing
implied repeal, on which the Petitioners
rely, do not determine the present issue.
In M. Karunanidhi, the Hon’ble
Supreme Court held that repeal by
implication cannot be inferred unless the
inconsistency between the enactments is
apparent and irreconcilable. In Union of
India v. Rajeev Bansal, the Hon’ble
Supreme Court reiterated that a later
enactment is not readily presumed to
repeal an earlier law in the absence of an
express or unambiguous intention and
that implied repeal must be avoided
where both enactments can operate
together. Those principles are well
settled. They have no application to the

- 303 -

AND 3 OTHERS
question whether the Legislature
expressly repealed the earlier forest law.
Section 117 answers that question in
clear terms. The issue here is the extent
of the savings made by that section.
71.12.2. The difference in nomenclature between
the Mysore Forest Regulation, 1900 and
the Mysore Forest Act, 1900 does not, by
itself, decide the issue. The notification
dated 05.02.1920 is described as having
been issued under Section 4 of the
Mysore Forest Regulation, 1900. Section
117 refers to the Mysore Forest Act,
1900, as in force in the Mysore Area.
The parties have proceeded on the basis
that the provision in Section 117 relates
to the earlier Mysore forest law
governing the notification in question.
For the purpose of deciding the present
point, the Court proceeds on that
common position. The second proviso to
Section 117 does not revive the
notification dated 05.02.1920. The
notification was duly published under the
old law. Its past operation is therefore

not erased by repeal. But the
Government subsequently dropped the
proposal to constitute Moorkangudda
Block as a State Forest by its order
dated 11.01.1924. As held under point
(i), that order brought the proposal to an
end. The notification was not in force on
01.06.1969, when the 1963 Act
commenced.
71.12.3. The expression “shall continue to be in
force accordingly” in the second proviso
presupposes that the action or
instrument was still in force when the
new Act came into operation. It does not
authorise the revival of an action which
had ceased to operate under the
repealed law. To hold otherwise would
convert a provision for continuity into a
power of revival. Section 117 contains
no such power. The general submission
of Sri Kiran Ron that actions validly
taken under the repealed enactment are
preserved is correct. It does not assist
the Respondents because the proposal
had already been withdrawn in 1924.

71.12.4. The first proviso must be considered
separately. It preserves the previous
operation of the repealed law, anything
duly done or suffered under it, rights,
privileges, obligations and liabilities
which accrued under it, and
investigations, proceedings and remedies
relating to them. It is therefore not
correct to say that the first proviso has
no application merely because the
notification was withdrawn before the
1963 Act commenced. Any right or
liability validly accrued under the
Regulation during its operation remains
governed by the first proviso, subject to
proof of its existence and legal character.
71.13. That saving, however, does not establish that a
proceeding under the Regulation remained
pending on 01.06.1969. The steps taken
between 1920 and 1923, preceded the
Government’s decision to drop the proposal. No
subsequent proceeding under the Regulation
has been identified. Nor have the Respondents
identified any investigation or legal proceeding

continued under the first proviso. The
proceedings commenced in 2022 and thereafter
were initiated under the 1963 Act, including
proceedings under Section 5 and the
succeeding provisions of Chapter II and action
under Section 64A. They were not a
continuation of any pending proceeding under
the Regulation. Their validity must therefore
rest on a notification under Section 4(1) of the
1963 Act or on a notification lawfully saved and
deemed to have been issued under the
corresponding provision by the second proviso
to Section 117. The withdrawn notification of
1920 provides neither.
71.14. The Petitioners also contend that a notification
under Section 4 of the Regulation, which merely
announced a proposal to constitute land as a
State Forest, cannot be treated as a notification
under Section 4(1) of the 1963 Act, which
requires a decision to constitute land as a
reserved forest. The distinction is apparent
from the language of the two provisions.
Section 4 of the Regulation applies when it is
proposed to constitute land as a State Forest
and requires the notification to declare that

when the State Government has decided to

requires the Forest Settlement Officer to be a

other than that of Forest Settlement Officer.
The Regulation, by contrast, states that the
officer shall ordinarily be a person other than a
Forest Officer.
71.15. The second proviso to Section 117 refers to a
“corresponding provision”, not an identical
provision. Whether a notification announcing a
proposal under the Regulation could, in a case
where it remained in force on the
commencement of the 1963 Act, be treated as
a notification under the corresponding provision
of that Act is a question which does not arise
for decision here. The notification dated
05.02.1920 had already ceased to operate in
1924.
71.16. It is sufficient to hold that Section 5 of the 1963
Act permits the Forest Settlement Officer to

publish a proclamation only when a notification
has been issued under Section 4 of that Act.
The Respondents have not established that a
notification under Section 4(1) of the 1963 Act
was issued in respect of Moorkangudda Block
before the proceedings commenced in 2022.
Nor can the withdrawn notification of 1920 be
treated as a notification continuing under
Section 117.
71.17. Sri Manmohan P.N.’s submission that the repeal
did not dispense with the need to complete the
statutory process is correct to the extent that
the State could not treat a preliminary proposal
as a final declaration of a State Forest or
reserved forest. The repeal itself did not
invalidate the notification dated 05.02.1920 or
erase its past operation. The proposal ceased to
operate because the Government dropped it by
its order dated 11.01.1924. The legal
consequence follows from that order, not from
the subsequent repeal of the Regulation.
71.18. The Respondents’ reliance on the savings
provision and the Petitioners’ reliance on the
distinction between the two enactments must

not revive the notification dated 05.02.1920,
which the Government had withdrawn in 1924.
Nor does it authorise the commencement of

that Section 117 of the Karnataka Forest Act,
1963, expressly repealed the earlier Mysore

extinguish the notification dated 05.02.1920 or
erase its past operation. The first proviso to

the repealed law and any rights, obligations,
liabilities, investigations, proceedings and
remedies validly accrued under it. The second

which remained in force when the 1963 Act
commenced, by deeming them to have been
taken under the corresponding provisions of

71.21. The proposal contained in the notification dated

not in force on 01.06.1969, when the 1963 Act

it as a continuing notification nor deems it to
have been issued under Section 4(1) of the

72. Answer to Point No. (iv): Whether the
prolonged lapse of time between the
preliminary notification and the subsequent
proceedings renders the proceedings legally
unsustainable on the grounds of unreasonable
delay, abandonment of the proposal to
constitute the lands as reserved forest, or
creation of third-party rights in the intervening
period?
72.1. Sri Manmohan P.N., learned counsel for the
Petitioners, submits in the alternative that,
even if the notification dated 05.02.1920 had
remained in force, the State was required to
initiate and complete the statutory proceedings
within a reasonable time. A delay of nearly a
century, without any satisfactory explanation, is
unreasonable and cannot sustain proceedings
commenced in 2022. The Regulation prescribes
a minimum period of three months for persons
to submit their claims after publication of the
proclamation under Section 5. Yet, no
proclamation or subsequent proceedings under

the Regulation followed the notification of 1920.
The prolonged inaction and failure to follow the
statutory procedure render the Respondents’
present reliance on the notification untenable.
72.2. Sri Manmohan P.N. relies on V. Sampangi
Ramaiah v. Director/Commissioner,
Department of Mines and Geology [2012
SCC OnLine Kar 8879], particularly paragraphs
10 to 21. He submits that, although the 1963
Act prescribes no period for publication of the
final notification under Section 17, the power
must be exercised within a reasonable time. In
paragraph 19, the Hon’ble Division bench of this
Court held that three years was a reasonable
period on the facts of that case, excluding the
time taken in acquisition proceedings, appeals
and court-ordered stays. In paragraph 21, the
Court held that the State had abandoned its
intention to constitute the land as a reserved
forest after taking no steps for over two
decades, particularly when a quarrying lease
had been granted in the meantime.
72.3. Sri Manmohan P.N. also relies on Joint
Collector, Ranga Reddy District v. D.

Narsing Rao [(2015) 3 SCC 695], particularly
paragraphs 25 to 31, for the principle that a
statutory power must be exercised within a
reasonable period even where the statute
prescribes no limitation. The length of the
delay, the explanation for it, the creation of
third-party rights, changes in the nature and
use of the property, and the need for finality in
transactions are relevant considerations. He
further relies on Union of India v. Citi Bank
[(2022) 19 SCC 188], particularly paragraph
19, for the proposition that, where the statute
prescribes no period, the authority must initiate
proceedings within a reasonable time having
regard to the facts and circumstances of the
case.
72.4. Sri A.M. Vijay, learned counsel for the
Petitioners in W.P. No. 29690/2025, submits
that when the Government decided not to
proceed with the proposal in 1924, the area
was sparsely populated. It is now inhabited and
developed, with agricultural activity, residential
structures and resorts. The owners have made
substantial investments in the lands. Acting
upon the notification after nearly a century

would cause grave prejudice to them. He
further submits that, before issuing a

the Forest Department ought to have assessed
the effect of the proposed action on the local
population. He also relies on the grants made
by the State in Kadumane Village between 1952
and 1961 as conduct consistent with the
Government having abandoned the proposal in
1924.
72.5. Sri Kiran Ron, learned Additional Advocate
General, submits that the Regulation prescribes
no outer time limit for publication of the final
notification under Section 17. The passage of
time cannot, by itself, extinguish the State’s
authority to protect lands covered by a forest
notification. He accepts that the statutory
process must be pursued within a reasonable
time but submits that what constitutes a
reasonable period depends on the
circumstances of each case, including the steps
taken by the authorities, the nature of the land
and the rights asserted by the Petitioners. He
further submits that the Respondents were

unable to proceed because of interim orders
passed by this Court.
72.6. Sri Kiran Ron further submits that the
Petitioners and their predecessors did not
question the notification for several decades
and approached this Court only after the Forest
Settlement Officer was appointed and
proceedings commenced. Their challenge is
therefore barred by delay, laches and
acquiescence. He submits that the ecological
importance of the area and its role in
maintaining forest and catchment functions are
matters of public interest which must be
considered while assessing the State’s action.
The passage of time should not defeat the
State’s lawful duty to protect the lands. He also
relies on the revenue records to contend that
the forest character of the lands was reflected
in Government records and that the Petitioners
acquired their properties with notice of those
entries and of the Forest Department’s claim.
According to him, the Petitioners cannot rely on
their purchases or subsequent investments to
claim equities against the State.

72.7. Sri Kiran Ron relies on M.C. Mehta v. Kamal
Nath [(1997) 1 SCC 388], in support of the
public trust doctrine. The principle, as explained
in the judgment, is that forests and other
natural resources are held by the Government
in trust for the public and that the doctrine
“enjoins upon the Government to protect the
resources for the enjoyment of the general
public rather than to permit their use for private
ownership or commercial purposes”. He submits
that the State, as trustee, must prevent
unauthorised appropriation, degradation and
exploitation of forest land. He accepts that this
obligation must be discharged within the
applicable statutory framework.
72.8. In support of the ecological importance of
Moorkangudda, Sri Kiran Ron refers to its
location in the Western Ghats, its biodiversity,
shola forests and grasslands, the streams
originating in the area, their connection with
the Cauvery and west-flowing river systems,
and the Yettinahole Integrated Drinking Water
Project. He also refers to the risk of ecological
damage from unauthorised occupation,
construction and commercial activity. He

accepts that claims concerning particular
species, and the course and hydrological
connectivity of the streams, must be
established through wildlife records and
scientific and hydrological material.
72.9. This point concerns three matters:
unreasonable delay, abandonment of the
proposal, and the creation of third-party rights
in the intervening period.
72.10. The finding under point (i) is that the
Government dropped the proposal contained in
the notification dated 05.02.1920 by its order
dated 11.01.1924. Points (ii) and (iii) hold that
the lands were never constituted a State Forest
under the Regulation and that the withdrawn
notification was not saved or revived by Section
117 of the 1963 Act. The Respondents
therefore cannot rely on the 1920 notification
as a continuing source of authority.
72.11. The question of delay and abandonment must
nevertheless be examined independently. In the
alternative, even if the notification had survived
the Government order of 11.01.1924, the State
could not leave the statutory process

- 318 -
 HC-KAR

unattended for nearly a century and then act on
the preliminary notification as though the
proceedings had remained pending throughout.
The effect of third-party rights is considered
separately.
72.12. The governing principle is settled. The absence
of a prescribed period of limitation does not
confer an unlimited power to act at any time. In
Joint Collector, Ranga Reddy District, the
Hon’ble Supreme Court held that “such power
must be exercised within a reasonable period”.
The Hon’ble Supreme Court also warned
against “endless uncertainty in human affairs”
and held that third-party rights created during
a prolonged and unexplained delay cannot be
defeated by a belated exercise of statutory
power. The reasonableness of the period
depends on the nature of the power, the
circumstances in which it is exercised and the
consequences of disturbing rights and
transactions which have attained finality.
72.13. In Union of India v. Citi Bank, the Hon’ble
Supreme Court reiterated in paragraph 19 that,
“where no such period has been provided in the

- 319 -
 HC-KAR

Statute, the authorities are required to initiate
the said proceeding within a reasonable period”.
What constitutes a reasonable period depends
on the facts and circumstances of the case.
72.14. This Hon’ble Division bench of this Court
applied the same principle to proceedings for
constitution of a reserved forest in V.
Sampangi Ramaiah. In paragraph 19, the
Hon’ble Division bench of this Court held that
the absence of a specific time limit for
publication of the notification under Section 17
of the 1963 Act required the final notification to
be issued within a reasonable time. Having
regard to the statutory scheme and the
circumstances of that case, the Hon’ble Division
bench considered three years to be a
reasonable period, excluding the time taken in
acquisition proceedings, appeals and court-ordered stays. In paragraph 21, it held that the
State had abandoned its intention to constitute
the land as a reserved forest after taking no
steps for over two decades, particularly when a
quarrying lease had been granted in the
meantime.

72.15. The three-year period fixed in Sampangi
Ramaiah is not a statutory period of limitation
applicable to every forest notification. It was
the reasonable period determined on the facts
of that case. The controlling principle is that
statutory proceedings must be pursued within a
reasonable time. That principle applies to the
present controversy as well. The Regulation
prescribes no outer time limit for completion of
the process under Section 17. That omission
does not authorise the State to leave a proposal
pending indefinitely.
72.16. The chronology is decisive. The notification
dated 05.02.1920 was published in the Mysore
Gazette on 12.02.1920. The Settlement Officer
submitted a report on 25.09.1922. The
Conservator of Forests reported against
constitution of the proposed forest in April
1923. The Government thereafter passed its
order dated 11.01.1924, the effect of which has
been determined under point (i).
72.17. Even on the Respondents’ case that the
notification dated 05.02.1920 remained in
force, the record discloses no further statutory

settlement proceedings for nearly a century. No
proclamation under Section 5 of the Regulation,
service of notices on the affected owners or
occupiers, completed inquiry into claims,
adjudication of rights, final demarcation or
notification under Section 17 has been
established for the period after the initial steps
in 1920 to 1923. The next material step was
the Government Order No. FW258AASC 2022
dated 19.12.2022 appointing the Forest
Settlement Officer to proceed with the matter.
The proclamation under Section 5 of the 1963
Act followed on 19.07.2023.
72.18. Thus, the State took no demonstrated step to
carry the statutory settlement process to
completion for nearly a century. The
Respondents have furnished no satisfactory
explanation for that prolonged inaction. The
interim orders passed by this Court do not
explain the delay. The order dated 10.08.2023,
on which the learned Additional Advocate
General relies, stayed proceedings under
Section 64A in W.P. No. 16251/2023. It was
passed after the appointment of the Forest
Settlement Officer in December 2022 and after

the proclamation of July 2023. It cannot explain
or justify the absence of proceedings between
1924 and 2022.
72.19. Sri Kiran Ron submits that the reasonableness
of the period must be assessed by considering
the steps taken by the authorities, the nature of
the lands and the rights claimed by the
Petitioners. Those considerations have been
examined. The steps taken between 1920 and
1923 did not lead to completion of the statutory
process. No subsequent settlement proceedings
have been shown until the appointment of the
Forest Settlement Officer in December 2022.
The State cannot explain a delay of nearly a
century by referring to steps taken at the
commencement of the process and then relying
on interim orders passed only in 2023.
72.20. The learned Additional Advocate General also
relies on the public interest in protecting the
area. The ecological and public interest
considerations are relevant to the State’s
decision to protect forests and other natural
resources. They do not displace the
requirement that statutory powers be exercised

within a reasonable time and in accordance
with law. Public interest cannot convert an
abandoned proposal into a perpetual source of
authority. The decision in Sampangi Ramaiah
itself concerned the proposed constitution of a
reserved forest. Despite the public interest in
forest protection, the Division Bench of this
Court held that the State’s prolonged inaction,
considered with the subsequent grant of a
quarrying lease, established abandonment.
72.21. The question of abandonment must be
considered in light of the State’s conduct. The
primary finding under point (i) is that the
Government expressly dropped the proposal on
11.01.1924. This is not a case in which
abandonment is inferred solely from the
passage of time. The Government’s own order
brought the proposal to an end.
72.22. Even if that finding is set aside and the
Respondents’ interpretation of the 1924 order is
accepted, the subsequent conduct of the
Government remains relevant. The Revenue
Department auctioned land in Sy. No. 92 of
Achanahalli Village in September 1924. The

Petitioners in W.P. No. 29690/2025 rely on
grants made by the State in Kadumane Village
between 1952 and 1961. The Respondents
themselves acknowledge that the Revenue
Department granted, auctioned or otherwise
dealt with lands in the area. These transactions
do not, by themselves, establish title or
determine whether the Revenue Department
was competent to deal with the lands.
72.23. Sri Kiran Ron submits that the Revenue
Department had no administrative control over
lands under the Forest Department and that the
auction and grants cannot bind the Forest
Department. He also points to the absence of
entries in the revenue records showing the
Forest Department’s name. These submissions
concern the authority of the Revenue
Department and the legal effect of its
transactions. They do not explain why the
statutory process under the Regulation was not
pursued for nearly a century.
72.24. The Respondents also rely on the mutation of
500 acres in Achanahalli Village in favour of the
Forest Department on 05.08.2013 and on

revenue records which, according to them,
show the Forest Department’s name in respect
of more than 6,400 acres. Those entries
evidence a later assertion of the Forest
Department’s claim over the lands covered by
the entries. They do not establish that the
statutory settlement process under the
Regulation was continued or completed. A
mutation or an entry in the Record of Rights is
not a substitute for the proclamation, inquiry,
adjudication and final notification prescribed by
the forest legislation. The Government Order
appointing the Forest Settlement Officer in
2022 is likewise a later step; it does not
retrospectively cure the prolonged failure to
pursue the statutory process within a
reasonable time.
72.25. The conclusion is therefore twofold. First, as
held under point (i), the Government expressly
dropped the proposal to constitute
Moorkangudda Block as a State Forest on
11.01.1924. Secondly, even if the notification
dated 05.02.1920 had survived that order, the
State’s failure to pursue the statutory process
for nearly a century, without any satisfactory

explanation, renders its belated reliance on the
notification legally unsustainable. The inaction,
viewed with the subsequent dealings with the
lands by Government authorities, establishes
abandonment of the proposal. A preliminary
notification cannot be kept in abeyance for an
indefinite period and acted upon after nearly a
century as though the statutory process had
remained alive. If the State now seeks to
constitute any land as a reserved forest, it must
proceed under the 1963 Act on the basis of a
fresh decision and the procedure prescribed
therein.
72.26. The objection that the Petitioners are
themselves guilty of delay, laches and
acquiescence is not sustainable. The Petitioners
do not challenge the validity of the notification
as it stood when issued in 1920. Their case is
that the Government withdrew the proposal in
1924 and that the Respondents cannot act upon
it after the lapse of nearly a century. The
immediate cause of action arose when the
Respondents began relying on the notification
to initiate proceedings, issue notices and take

coercive action between 2022 and 2025. The
petitions were filed in response to those steps.
72.27. The principle discussed in paragraph 30 of
Joint Collector, Ranga Reddy District, with
reference to Dehri Rohtas Light Railway Co.
Ltd. v. District Board, Bhojpur, requires the
Court to consider whether the Petitioner
approached the Court before a parallel right
was created and whether the delay is
attributable to the Petitioner’s own negligence
or inaction. The Petitioners approached this
Court after the Respondents began acting upon
the 1920 notification. The long period during
which the State itself took no statutory step
cannot be attributed to the Petitioners. Their
challenge to the present reliance on the
notification is not barred by laches.
72.28. The effect of the Petitioners’ participation in
proceedings before the Forest Settlement
Officer, and whether any individual Petitioner is
precluded from raising a particular objection by
reason of such participation, is considered
under point (vii).

72.29. The third aspect concerns third-party rights.
The Petitioners rely on grants, sale deeds,
revenue entries, houses, agricultural activity,
resorts, licences and substantial investments
made over the years. Sri Kiran Ron disputes the
equities claimed by the Petitioners and submits
that they acquired their properties with notice
of the Forest Department’s claim and the
relevant revenue entries.
72.30. The creation of third-party rights and changes
in the nature and use of property are relevant
to the application of the rule of reasonable
time. Joint Collector, Ranga Reddy District
recognises that transactions which have
attained finality and rights created in favour of
third parties cannot be unsettled by an
unexplained and belated exercise of statutory
power. Those considerations assume
significance in the present case.
72.31. This Court does not, under this point,
adjudicate the title or the enforceability of the
rights claimed by any individual Petitioner or
purchaser. Whether a particular grant, auction,
sale deed, revenue entry or other instrument

- 329 -

AND 3 OTHERS
confers a legally enforceable right must be
determined on its own terms and under the
applicable law. The question whether a
purchaser acquired property with notice of the
Forest Department’s claim is also a matter to be
examined on the relevant evidence. Those
questions are considered under point (v). The
existence and extent of individual properties
within the notified boundaries are considered
under point (vi).
72.32. The finding on delay and abandonment does
not rest on the equities of any individual
purchaser. The State’s own order of 1924 and
its prolonged failure to pursue the statutory
process provide the basis for the conclusion.
The intervening transactions, changes in land
use and investments reinforce the prejudice
that would result from permitting the State to
act upon the proposal after such an
extraordinary lapse of time.
72.33. The public trust doctrine is an established
principle of law. In M.C. Mehta v. Kamal
Nath, the Hon’ble Supreme Court recognised
that the Government holds forests, rivers and

- 330 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024

AND 3 OTHERS
other natural resources in trust for the public
and must protect them for public enjoyment.
The doctrine imposes a duty on the State; it
does not confer a statutory power which the
Legislature has withheld or permit the State to
act without authority of law.
72.34. Article 300A of the Constitution of India
provides:
“No person shall be deprived of his property save by
authority of law.”
72.35. The public trust doctrine and Article 300A must
operate together. The State must protect
natural resources, but it must do so by
exercising powers conferred by law and in
accordance with the procedure prescribed by
law. The duty to protect forests cannot justify
proceedings founded on a notification which
had been withdrawn or on a statutory proposal
left unattended for nearly a century. Equally,
this judgment does not release any land which
is otherwise established to be forest land from
the protection of the applicable law. The State
remains under a duty to protect such land
through lawful measures.

72.36. This Court has considered the ecological
submissions advanced by Sri Kiran Ron. The
location of Moorkangudda in the Western Ghats,
the presence of shola forests and grasslands,
the biodiversity of the area and the role of its
streams and catchments are matters of public
importance. The learned Additional Advocate
General has himself accepted that species-specific claims and the hydrological connections
of the streams must be assessed on wildlife
records and scientific material.
72.37. This Court makes no finding on the ecological
character of any particular parcel or on the
asserted hydrological connection with the
Yettinahole Integrated Drinking Water Project.
72.38. The ecological importance of the area does not
alter the legal conclusion on delay. It requires
the State to act promptly and lawfully to
protect any land established to be forest land.
It does not authorise the State to proceed on a
withdrawn or abandoned proposal.
72.39. The State remains free to take action under the
applicable forest legislation, including by
initiating proceedings under Section 4(1) of the

1963 Act where the statutory conditions are
satisfied. Nothing in this judgment protects any
construction, clearing, felling or commercial
activity which is otherwise unlawful.
72.40. This Court answers Point No. (iv) by holding
that the Government dropped the proposal
contained in the notification dated 05.02.1920
to constitute Moorkangudda Block as a State
Forest by its order dated 11.01.1924. This is
the primary ground on which the Respondents
cannot rely upon that notification as a
continuing basis for proceedings against the
Petitioners.
72.41. In the alternative, even if the notification had
survived the Government order, the State’s
failure to pursue the statutory process for
nearly a century, without any satisfactory
explanation, constitutes unreasonable delay
and establishes abandonment of the proposal.
The subsequent appointment of the Forest
Settlement Officer and commencement of
proceedings in 2022 and 2023 do not cure that
delay. The State cannot act upon the 1920

notification after leaving the statutory process
unattended for such an extraordinary period.
72.42. The objections of delay, laches and
acquiescence raised against the Petitioners are
rejected. The State remains at liberty to take
fresh action in accordance with the 1963 Act
and other applicable law, upon satisfying the

“the KLR Act”), which provides:
“133. Presumption regarding entries in the

that the Petitioners rely on grant certificates,
registered sale deeds and partition deeds as the
source of their respective rights.
73.5. Sri Kiran Ron, learned Additional Advocate

notwithstanding the notification dated

73.7. In the alternative, and without prejudice to

not to the entire extent of 14 acres 36 guntas

73.8. For all the petitions, Sri Kiran Ron relies on
Section 6 of the Regulation and Section 6 of the

the notification under Section 4, rights cannot
be acquired in the notified lands except in the

Claims based on subsequent grants and

73.9. He further contends that the Revenue

Department and was not competent to grant or

authority lacking competence does not create
an indefeasible right against the State merely
because it is followed by possession or reflected
in revenue records. In several instances,
according to him, the Revenue Department

dealt with lands claimed by the Forest
Department without obtaining its concurrence.
73.10. Sri Kiran Ron also relies on the entries showing
the Forest Department’s name in Columns 9 or
11 of the Records of Rights, Tenancy and Crops
(RTCs) relating to more than 6,400 acres. He
submits that the Petitioners have not
established the source of their title or traced
the chain of devolution through successive
holders. A purchaser cannot acquire a better
title than that held by the transferor. The State,
he submits, is not bound by grants, permissions
or revenue entries which are contrary to law or
made by authorities lacking competence. The
Petitioners cannot claim an enforceable right or
equity merely from acts of public authorities
which were beyond their powers.
73.11. Sri Kiran Ron relies on State of Karnataka v.
I.S. Nirvane Gowda [(2007) 15 SCC 744],
particularly paragraph 4, to submit that, once
land is included in a reserved forest, revenue
entries are of no consequence, saguvali chits do
not confer title, and the Revenue Authorities
are not competent to deal with such land.

73.12. He also relies on Prabhagiya Van Adhikari
Awadh Van Prabhag v. Arun Kumar
Bhardwaj [(2021) 18 SCC 104], particularly
paragraphs 24 and 28, to submit that, while a
preliminary notification remains in force, rights
cannot be acquired except through a written
contract made by or on behalf of the
Government, and a lessee cannot claim rights
merely on the basis of a revenue entry without
a written agreement executed by a competent
authority.
73.13. Sri Kiran Ron further relies on Girish H.E. v.
State of Karnataka [2013 SCC OnLine Kar
10537], particularly paragraph 19, concerning
the effect of Section 6 of the 1963 Act on
grants made after publication of a notification
under Section 4. He also relies on Jit Ram Shiv
Kumar v. State of Haryana [(1981) 1 SCC
11], particularly paragraph 51, for the
proposition that there can be no promissory
estoppel against the exercise of legislative
power or against the Government discharging
its statutory duties. The Government, he
submits, is not bound by acts of officers who
acted beyond the scope of their authority.

73.14. Lastly, he relies on Section 2 of the 1980 Act
and contends that certain diversions and grants
made after its commencement were effected
without the prior approval of the Central
Government.
73.15. This point does not require the Court to declare
the title of any Petitioner. The title to each
property depends on the documents relied
upon, the authority of the person who made the
original grant, the extent of the land granted
and the subsequent chain of transactions.
Those matters cannot be finally determined in
these writ petitions on the basis of affidavits,
particularly when the documents and the rights
of persons who are not parties may also be
involved.
73.16. The question for determination is whether the
forest legislation or any want of competence
established on the present record renders the
documents relied upon by the Petitioners legally
ineffective. The answer requires examination of
the statutory restrictions, the decisions relied
upon by the Respondents, and the legal effect
of each category of document.

- 341 -
 HC-KAR

73.17. The Respondents rely on Section 6(1) of the
Regulation to contend that no rights could be
acquired in the notified area after publication of
the notification under Section 4. The relevant
portion of Section 6(1), reads:
“(1) During the interval between the publication of
such proclamation and the date fixed by the
notification declaring the forest to be a State Forest
as hereinafter provided, no right shall be acquired in
or over the land comprised in such notification,
except by succession, or under a grant or contract
in writing made or entered into by, or on behalf of,
the Government or some person in whom such
right, or power to create such right, was vested
when the proclamation was published; … No right of
occupancy shall, without the previous sanction of
the Government, be granted in respect of such land,
and every right of occupancy granted without such
sanction shall be null and void.”
73.18. The language of Section 6(1) is clear. The
prohibition on the acquisition of rights does not
commence merely upon publication of the
notification under Section 4. It operates during
the interval between publication of the
proclamation under Section 5 and the date
fixed by the final notification declaring the
forest to be a State Forest. The provision also
recognises exceptions for acquisition of rights
by succession and under a written grant or
contract made by or on behalf of the

- 342 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024

WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
Government, or by a person vested with the
power to create such rights when the
proclamation was published. A right of
occupancy, however, cannot be granted without
the previous sanction of the Government.
73.19. The Respondents have not produced a
proclamation under Section 5 of the Regulation
in respect of Moorkangudda Block. The burden
of establishing the statutory bar rests on the
Respondents, who rely upon it to defeat the
rights claimed by the Petitioners. In the
absence of proof of the event which attracts
Section 6(1), that provision cannot be invoked
to invalidate the grants and transactions relied
upon by the Petitioners.
73.20. There is a further and decisive consideration.
The Government withdrew the proposal to
constitute Moorkangudda Block as a State
Forest by its order dated 11.01.1924. As held
under point (i), the proposal did not remain
alive thereafter. The statutory process initiated
by the proposal thus came to an end. The
restrictions attached to that process cannot be
treated as continuing indefinitely after the

- 343 -

Government itself had abandoned it. The
auction in Achanahalli Village took place in
September 1924, after the Government order
of 11.01.1924. The grants relied upon in
Kadumane Village were made between 1952
and 1961. Neither category of transaction is
rendered invalid by Section 6(1) of the
Regulation on the basis of the withdrawn
proposal.
73.21. Section 6(1) of the 1963 Act provides that,
after publication of a notification under Section
4, no right shall be acquired in or over the land
comprised in such notification, except by
succession or under a grant or contract in
writing made by or on behalf of the
Government or a person vested with the power
to create such right. The provision also requires
the previous sanction of the State Government
for the grant of a right of occupancy.
73.22. The restriction under the 1963 Act therefore
depends upon the issue of a notification under
Section 4 of that Act. No such notification has
been issued in respect of Moorkangudda Block.
Nor was the notification dated 05.02.1920

carried into the 1963 Act by Section 117, as
held under point (iii). Section 6 of the 1963 Act
is consequently not attracted to the Petitioners’
lands on the basis of the 1920 notification.
73.23. The decision in Girish H.E., particularly
paragraph 19, concerns rights in land covered
by a notification under Section 4 of the 1963
Act. It recognises the restrictions imposed by
Section 6 and also the exceptions which that
provision permits. It does not support the
application of Section 6 to lands which are not
covered by a subsisting notification under that
Act.
73.24. The Respondents’ reliance on I.S. Nirvane
Gowda must be considered in its factual and
legal context. In that case, the trial court and
the first appellate court had, on the evidence,
found that the lands formed part of a reserved
forest. The Hon’ble Supreme Court held:
“When the lands were included in reserve forest, the
entries in the revenue records were of no
consequence and further, mere saguvali chits did
not confer any title on the suit lands. This apart, the
Revenue Authorities were not competent to deal
with the property which was the part of the
reserved forest.”

- 345 -

73.25. The principle laid down by the Hon’ble Supreme
Court is that once land is duly established to
form part of a reserved forest, revenue entries
and saguvali chits cannot override that status,
and the Revenue Authorities cannot deal with
the land contrary to the forest legislation.
73.26. The decision does not apply to the present case
in the manner suggested by the Respondents.
Under point (ii), this Court has held that
Moorkangudda Block was never constituted a
State Forest under the Regulation and did not
acquire the status of a reserved forest under
the 1963 Act. The Respondents cannot
therefore invoke I.S. Nirvane Gowda on the
assumption that the Petitioners’ lands form part
of a reserved forest. Whether any parcel is
forest land on an independent basis, including
under the 1980 Act, remains open.
73.27. The Respondents’ further submission that the
Revenue Department lacked competence to
deal with the lands presupposes that the
relevant lands were under the control of the
Forest Department or had otherwise been
placed beyond the authority of the Revenue

Department under a subsisting statutory
provision. The withdrawn notification of 1920
does not establish that proposition. The Forest
Department’s revenue entries must be
examined on their own evidentiary basis, as
considered below. The competence of the
authority which issued any particular grant
must also be assessed with reference to the
relevant grant, the extent and identity of the
land, and the law in force on the date of the
grant.
73.28. In Prabhagiya Van Adhikari Awadh Van
Prabhag v. Arun Kumar Bhardwaj, the
Hon’ble Supreme Court held in paragraph 24
that, even in the absence of a final notification,
“by virtue of Section 5 of the Forest Act, there
is a prohibition against acquisition of any right
over the land comprised in such notification
except by way of a contract executed in writing
by or on behalf of the Government”. In
paragraph 28, the Hon’ble Supreme Court held
that the claimant had to establish the right
through “an agreement in writing by a
competent authority” and could not claim a

right merely on the basis of an entry in the
revenue records.
73.29. The principle that a person claiming a right
under a statutory forest notification must
establish the written grant or agreement
required by the applicable law is sound. The
statutory provision considered in Arun Kumar
Bhardwaj, however, was Section 5 of the
Indian Forest Act, 1927, as amended in Uttar
Pradesh. As already explained under point (ii),
that provision is not identical to Section 6(1) of
the Regulation. The decision also proceeded on
the basis of a subsisting preliminary notification
and an express statutory bar on the acquisition
of rights.
73.30. Neither premise is established here. The
proposal contained in the 1920 notification was
withdrawn on 11.01.1924, and the Respondents
have not established publication of the
proclamation required to attract Section 6(1) of
the Regulation. The auction in Achanahalli
Village took place after the proposal had been
withdrawn. The decision in Arun Kumar
Bhardwaj therefore does not render that

auction or the subsequent grants invalid merely
because they relate to lands once covered by
the 1920 proposal.
73.31. The documents relating to the auction in favour
of Sri Byregowda include the auction
advertisement dated 30.08.1924, the auction
proceedings, the bid amount, the payment
made into the Government treasury and the
supplemental khetwar recording a grant of five
acres in original Survey No. 95, subsequently
re-surveyed as Survey No. 92. The Petitioners
rely on these documents to establish the source
of their claim.
73.32. Sri Kiran Ron, in the alternative, submits that
the right derived from the auction extends to
five acres only and not to the entire extent of
14 acres 36 guntas. That submission is
consistent with the extent of five acres
recorded in the supplemental khetwar. The
Petitioners in W.P. No. 16251/2023 also claim
the property as measuring five acres.
73.33. The auction proceedings produced by the
Petitioners record the date of auction as
20.09.1924. The reference to 29.09.1924 is not

borne out by the auction record placed before
this Court. To the extent that the learned
Additional Advocate General has referred to the
latter date, the same is not supported by the
documentary record produced in relation to the
auction. In any event, the auction is
subsequent to the Government decision dated
11.01.1924. The legal effect, genuineness and
precise extent of the grant, and the title derived
by subsequent transferees, remain matters to
be established on the documents in the
appropriate proceedings.
73.34. In Jit Ram Shiv Kumar v. State of Haryana,
the Hon’ble Supreme Court held in paragraph
51:
“There can be no promissory estoppel against the
exercise of legislative power of the State.”
73.35. The Hon’ble Supreme Court further held that
the doctrine cannot be invoked “for preventing
the Government from acting in discharge of its
duty under the law” and that “the Government
would not be bound by the act of its officers
and agents who act beyond the scope of their
authority”.

73.36. The Petitioners’ case, however, does not rest on
promissory estoppel. They rely on Government
grants, auction documents and other
instruments as sources of claimed rights. They
also rely on the Government’s order dated
11.01.1924 as the basis for contending that the
proposal to constitute the Block as a State
Forest had been withdrawn. The legal effect of
those documents must be determined under
the applicable statutory provisions and cannot
be rejected merely by invoking the doctrine of
promissory estoppel.
73.37. Whether a particular Revenue Officer acted
beyond the authority conferred by law is a
matter to be determined with reference to the
particular grant and the law in force when it
was made. The Respondents have not
identified, in respect of each grant relied upon,
the specific statutory prohibition or want of
authority which rendered it invalid. Their
general submission that the Revenue
Department lacked competence cannot, by
itself, invalidate every grant or transaction in
the area. Nothing in this judgment prevents the
State from establishing, in the appropriate

proceedings, that a particular grant was made
by an authority lacking competence or in
breach of a statutory prohibition.
73.38. The legal effect of the different categories of
documents relied upon by the Petitioners must
now be considered separately.
GRANTS BY THE GOVERNMENT, INCLUDING THE
1924 AUCTION AND THE SAGUVALI CHITS ISSUED
BETWEEN 1952 AND 1961:
73.39. The 1924 auction took place after the
Government had withdrawn the proposal to
constitute Moorkangudda Block as a State
Forest. The saguvali chits relied upon by the
Petitioners in W.P. No. 29690/2025 were issued
between 1952 and 1961, long after the
withdrawal. The objection that these grants
were invalid merely because the lands had been
covered by the 1920 notification is therefore
rejected.
73.40. This does not amount to a declaration that
every such grant is valid. The claimant must
establish the genuineness of the grant, the
authority of the officer who issued it, the

- 352 -
 HC-KAR

conditions attached to it, the extent granted
and the identity of the land. The legal effect of
each grant must be determined on its own
terms and under the law applicable on the date
of its issue.
73.41. The Respondents submit that the Petitioner in
W.P. No. 29656/2024 has not produced
sufficient material to establish a grant by the
State in favour of the Petitioner or its
predecessors. The burden of establishing the
source of title rests on that Petitioner. This
Court makes no finding that the Petitioner has
established a Government grant or a complete
chain of title.
REVENUE ENTRIES AND MUTATIONS:
73.42. Revenue entries are evidence of the matters
recorded in them; they are not, by themselves,
instruments of title. Section 133 of the KLR Act
provides a rebuttable presumption in favour of
an entry in the Record of Rights and a certified
entry in the Register of Mutations or patta
book. The presumption continues until the
contrary is proved or a new entry is lawfully
substituted.

- 353 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
73.43. The presumption applies to qualifying entries
relied upon by the Petitioners. It also applies to
qualifying entries in favour of the Forest
Department. The Respondents rely on the
mutation made on 05.08.2013 in respect of
land in Achanahalli Village and on RTC entries
showing the Forest Department’s name in
Columns 9 or 11 in respect of more than 6,400
acres.
73.44. The presumption under Section 133 is not
conclusive and cannot determine the competing
claims merely by the existence of entries on
either side. The source, date, certification, legal
basis and extent of each entry must be
examined. An entry in favour of the Forest
Department does not, without more, establish
that the land was duly constituted a reserved
forest or that the Department acquired control
over it under a statutory provision. Equally, an
entry in the Petitioners’ names does not, by
itself, establish that the State granted the land
or that the person whose name appears in the
record had title capable of transfer.

73.45. The Respondents are correct that revenue
entries cannot, by themselves, determine the
legal status of land. The same principle applies
to the entries on which they rely. This Court
makes no finding on the correctness of the
competing entries or on the extent of land
covered by them. Those matters must be
determined with reference to the original
records and the identity of the respective
parcels.
REGISTERED SALE DEEDS, PARTITION DEEDS AND
OTHER INSTRUMENTS OF TRANSFER:
73.46. A registered sale deed or partition deed
transfers only the interest which the transferor
lawfully possesses. A purchaser cannot acquire
a better title than that of the transferor. The
Respondents are correct in relying on this
principle.
73.47. The Petitioners must therefore establish the
original source of title and trace the devolution
of the claimed rights through the successive
holders. The production of a sale deed or
partition deed, without proof of the transferor’s
title, does not establish ownership against the

- 355 -
 HC-KAR

WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
State. The sufficiency of the chain of title must
be examined separately for each Petitioner and
each parcel. This Court does not decide that
issue in these proceedings.
73.48. In W.P. No. 16251/2023, the Petitioners rely on
the 1924 auction in favour of Sri Byregowda
and subsequent instruments of transfer,
including the registered sale deed and partition
deed. The Respondents’ alternative submission
is that the original auction covered five acres
only. The documents relied upon also refer to a
grant of five acres. The claim cannot, therefore,
be enlarged beyond the extent supported by
the original grant merely on the basis of
subsequent transactions or revenue entries.
Whether the subsequent transfers validly
conveyed the interest originally granted
remains to be established in the appropriate
proceedings.
CONVERSION ORDERS, LICENCES,
REGISTRATIONS AND OTHER PERMISSIONS:
73.49. A conversion order permitting non-agricultural
use, a licence to establish or operate a resort,
registration with a statutory authority, a No

Objection Certificate, a Panchayat approval or a
tax entry is issued for the limited purpose
governed by the relevant statute. Such an
order or permission does not, by itself, confer
ownership or establish the title of the person in
whose favour it is issued.
73.50. The Petitioners rely, among other documents,
on the conversion order dated 12.06.2019 in
W.P. No. 16251/2023, the conversion order
dated 13.06.2014 in W.P. No. 29656/2024, and
approvals or permissions issued by the Zilla
Panchayat, the Karnataka State Pollution
Control Board and the Panchayat Development
Officer. These documents may establish that
the concerned authorities permitted particular
activities or uses of the land. They do not
establish the underlying title or cure a defect in
the title of the applicant.
73.51. The Respondents have not identified a specific
statutory defect in each of these permissions or
established that the authority which issued any
particular permission lacked competence. Their
validity must be examined with reference to the
relevant statute, the status of the land and the

authority of the issuing officer. This Court does
not declare any of these permissions valid or
invalid. Nor do they confer a right to continue
any activity which is prohibited by the
applicable forest or environmental legislation.
THE FOREST (CONSERVATION) ACT, 1980:
73.52. The Respondents rely on Section 2 of the 1980
Act. The relevant provision, as extracted above,
restricts the State Government or any other
authority from making, without the prior
approval of the Central Government, an order
directing:
“(i) that any reserved forest (within the meaning of
the expression ‘reserved forest’ in any law for the
time being in force in that State) or any portion
thereof, shall cease to be reserved;
(ii) that any forest land or any portion thereof may
be used for any non-forest purpose.”
73.53. The provision also regulates the assignment of
forest land by way of lease or otherwise to
private persons or other entities, subject to the
statutory requirements.
73.54. The 1980 Act came into force on 25.10.1980.
The auction of 1924 and the saguvali chits
issued between 1952 and 1961 predate its

- 358 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
commencement. Section 2 of the 1980 Act
cannot retrospectively invalidate those grants
merely because they relate to land which the
State now claims to be forest land.
73.55. The position is different in respect of orders
made after the commencement of the 1980
Act. If any parcel is established to be forest
land within the meaning of that Act, the use of
that land for a non-forest purpose or its
assignment by the State or other authority
must comply with Section 2 and obtain the
prior approval of the Central Government where
the provision so requires. A conversion order or
other permission issued by a State authority
cannot override that statutory requirement.
73.56. The question whether any particular parcel
constitutes forest land within the meaning of
the 1980 Act has been left open under point
(ii). The Respondents must establish that status
with reference to the relevant records and the
nature of the land. The mere fact that the land
was once included in the proposal under the
1920 notification does not establish that status,
since the proposal was withdrawn in 1924.

- 359 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
Equally, the absence of a final notification under
Section 17 of the Regulation does not, by itself,
exclude land from the wider meaning of forest
under the 1980 Act.
73.57. This Court therefore makes no finding on
whether any post-25.10.1980 conversion order,
grant, assignment or other permission
concerning a particular parcel contravened
Section 2 of the 1980 Act. The State is at
liberty to examine any such transaction and
take action authorised by that Act, provided it
first establishes that the land falls within its
scope and complies with the statutory
procedure.
73.58. The grants, saguvali chits and other
instruments relied upon by the Petitioners are
not rendered invalid merely because the lands
were once covered by the notification dated
05.02.1920. That proposal was withdrawn by
the Government order dated 11.01.1924. The
notification was not saved or revived by Section
117 of the 1963 Act. The Respondents have
also not established publication of a
proclamation under Section 5 of the Regulation

- 360 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024

so as to attract the prohibition under Section
6(1). Section 6 of the 1963 Act is not attracted
because no notification under Section 4 of that
Act, or notification deemed to have been issued
under it, is in force in respect of the Block.
73.59. This conclusion does not validate every grant,
sale deed, revenue entry, conversion order or
permission relied upon by the Petitioners. Each
document must be proved and its legal effect
determined with reference to its source, the
authority of the person who issued or executed
it, the extent of land covered and the applicable
law. Revenue entries are rebuttable evidence
and do not create title. A conveyance transfers
only the interest held by the transferor.
Conversion orders and licences do not confer
ownership.
73.60. The restrictions under Section 2 of the 1980 Act
continue to apply to any parcel established to
be forest land within the meaning of that Act.
The validity of post-25.10.1980 diversions,
assignments and permissions must be tested
against that provision. This Court does not
declare title in favour of any Petitioner or

AND 3 OTHERS
determine the forest character of any individual
parcel under the 1980 Act.
73.61. In W.P. No. 16251/2023, the Respondents
themselves submit in the alternative that the
right arising from the auction extends to five
acres only. The auction records and
supplemental khetwar relied upon by the
Petitioners also refer to five acres. The claim
under the auction cannot extend beyond that
extent on the basis of the documents presently
identified. This observation does not amount to
a declaration of title in favour of the Petitioners.
73.62. This Court answers Point No. (v) by holding that
the grants, saguvali chits, registered
instruments, revenue entries, conversion
orders, licences and permissions relied upon by
the Petitioners are not rendered ineffective
merely by the notification dated 05.02.1920, by
Section 6 of the Mysore Forest Regulation,
1900, or by Section 6 of the Karnataka Forest
Act, 1963. The proposal contained in the 1920
notification was withdrawn by the Government
order dated 11.01.1924 and was not saved or
revived under Section 117 of the 1963 Act. The

Respondents have not established publication of

73.63. The documents relied upon by the Petitioners
are not, however, conclusive proof of title. Their
genuineness, extent, the competence of the
issuing authority and the chain of devolution
must be established in respect of each property.
Revenue entries, whether in the Petitioners’
names or in the name of the Forest
Department, are rebuttable evidence and do
not, by themselves, create or extinguish title. A
sale deed or partition deed transfers only the
interest lawfully held by the transferor.
Conversion orders, licences and permissions do
not confer ownership.
73.64. Section 2 of the Forest (Conservation) Act,
1980, continues to apply to any parcel
established to be forest land within the
meaning of that Act. The validity of any post-25.10.1980 diversion, assignment or

75.6. In W.P. No. 33767/2025, he states that 86 of

75.7. The objection raised by the Respondents is
described as one of maintainability. The
distinction between maintainability and
entertainability is material. The availability of
an alternative remedy does not take away the
jurisdiction conferred on this Court by Article
226 of the Constitution. It is a settled rule of
judicial restraint that the Court will ordinarily
decline to entertain a writ petition when an
effective and efficacious statutory remedy is
available. That rule, however, is one of
discretion and not an absolute bar to the
exercise of jurisdiction.
75.8. In M/s. Godrej Sara Lee Ltd. (paragraph 4),
the Hon’ble Supreme Court reiterated that the

- 381 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
rule requiring a party to pursue an alternative
remedy is a rule of policy, convenience and
discretion rather than a rule of law. The Court
also emphasised that maintainability and
entertainability are distinct concepts. In
paragraph 6, it reiterated the recognised
exceptions to the rule, including cases in which
the proceedings are wholly without jurisdiction.
In Radha Krishan Industries (paragraph 27),
the Hon’ble Supreme Court restated the
governing principles. Paragraph 27.3 identifies
the recognised exceptions, including violation of
natural justice and proceedings wholly without
jurisdiction. Paragraph 27.4 clarifies that the
existence of an alternative remedy does not
divest the High Court of its jurisdiction.
Paragraph 27.5, however, emphasises that
where a statute creates a right and provides a
remedy for enforcing it, the party must
ordinarily pursue that remedy. Paragraph 27.6
recognises that disputed questions of fact may
also justify a refusal to entertain a writ petition.
75.9. These principles govern the present cases. The
existence of an alternative remedy must be
examined with reference to the nature of the

particular action challenged, the relief sought
and the grounds raised. The objection cannot
be decided uniformly without distinguishing
between challenges to the foundational
notification, statutory proclamations and
notices, proceedings under Section 64A, and
orders determining individual claims.
75.10. The principal jurisdictional challenge in these
petitions concerns the legal foundation on
which the Respondents initiated the
proceedings. The Petitioners rely on the
notification dated 05.02.1920 and the
Government Order dated 11.01.1924. The
Respondents contend that the notification of
1920 continued to remain in force
notwithstanding the Government Order. The
legal effect of these instruments, the absence
of a final notification under Section 17 of the
Mysore Forest Regulation, 1900, and the
consequences of repeal under Section 117 of
the Karnataka Forest Act, 1963, have been
considered and decided under Points (i) to (iv).
75.11. This Court has held that the Government Order
dated 11.01.1924 dropped the proposal to

- 383 -

constitute the entire Moorkangudda Block as a
State Forest. It has also held that the
withdrawn proposal cannot be revived through
the repeal-and-savings provision in Section 117
of the 1963 Act. The absence of a final
notification under Section 17 and the absence
of any subsisting action saved by Section 117
have also been considered. The Respondents
cannot, therefore, rely on the withdrawn
proposal of 1920, by itself, as a subsisting
statutory foundation for the impugned action.
75.12. The challenge to the legal effect of these
instruments is not a dispute requiring the
adjudication of title or the resolution of
contested evidence. It turns on the
interpretation of the statutory provisions and
the legal effect of documents whose existence
and contents are not in dispute. The Forest
Settlement Officer cannot conclusively
determine the legal effect of the Government
Order or the continued existence of the very
notification from which his authority is claimed
to derive. The challenge therefore raises a
question going to the root of the authority to
initiate proceedings on the basis of the 1920

by itself, determine whether any independent
action may be taken under another applicable
provision of law.
75.13. The statutory remedies must also be examined
according to their scope. Section 16(1) of the
Karnataka Forest Act, 1963, provides an appeal
to the Karnataka Appellate Tribunal by a person
who has made a claim under Chapter II, or by
an authorised Forest Officer or other person,
against an order passed by the Forest
Settlement Officer on that claim under Section
11, Section 12 or Section 14. The appeal must
be filed within three months from the date of
the order. Section 16(2) provides a separate
appeal to the District Court against an order
under Section 15.
75.14. The remedy under Section 16 is thus directed
against an order determining a claim under the
specified provisions. It does not provide an
appeal against the notification, the appointment
of the Forest Settlement Officer, a proclamation
under Section 5 or a notice calling upon a

- 385 -
 HC-KAR

person to appear and produce documents. The
statutory appeal cannot, therefore, be treated
as a remedy against every action taken during
the course of the forest-settlement
proceedings.
75.15. The principal challenges in these writ petitions
include the proclamations dated 19.07.2023
and 09.11.2023; the notices issued by the
Forest Settlement Officer, including those dated
04.06.2024, 28.08.2024, 10.09.2024,
13.02.2025, 26.06.2025, 30.06.2025 and
07.07.2025; the notices issued by the Assistant
Conservator of Forests and the Deputy
Conservator of Forests; and the notices issued
in the A.Mo. series. These actions are not
orders deciding claims under Sections 11, 12 or
14. Section 16 does not provide an appeal
against them. The Respondents have not
identified any other provision under which an
appeal against the proclamations or the notices
themselves could be pursued.
75.16. The Respondents also rely on orders passed by
the Forest Settlement Officer on individual
claims. The order dated 24.10.2025 in W.P. No.

29656/2024 recognised the Petitioner's claim to
5 acres and 38 guntas and rejected the claim to
a further extent of 11 acres and 18 guntas.
That order was passed after the writ petition
had been instituted. The prayers in W.P. No.
29656/2024 do not seek to quash or review
that order. The Petitioner has not amended the
prayers to bring that order under challenge. Nor
does the Petitioner seek, in these proceedings,
a reappraisal of the documents or a
determination of the claims accepted or
rejected by the Forest Settlement Officer. The
correctness of that order is, therefore, not in
issue in this writ petition and must be pursued
in accordance with Section 16.
75.17. In W.P. No. 33767/2025, the prayers challenge
the proclamations and notices and seek
consequential relief. They do not specifically
seek to quash an order passed by the Forest
Settlement Officer under Section 11, Section 12
or Section 14. The learned Additional Advocate
General refers to the claims admitted or
rejected in the course of the proceedings. The
existence, date and service of any appealable
order on each such claim must, however, be
