distinguished from the report stated to have
been prepared and forwarded to other
authorities. A report by itself is not an order
determining a claim under Section 11, Section
12 or Section 14. Where an appealable order
has in fact been passed, the remedy against its
merits lies under Section 16.
75.18. The availability of that remedy does not prevent
this Court from examining the legality of the
proclamations and notices which are the subject
of the prayers.
75.19. The Respondents' contention that the
Petitioners seek adjudication of disputed claims
in these writ petitions cannot be accepted as a
ground for declining to examine the
jurisdictional issues. This Court is not called
upon under this Point to determine the title of
any Petitioner, the extent of land lawfully held
by any Petitioner, the validity of individual
grants or transactions, or the evidentiary value
of the documents relied upon by the parties.
Those matters remain open for consideration
before the competent statutory authority and,
where an appeal lies, before the appellate

forum. The issues addressed here concern the
legal authority to initiate the impugned
proceedings and the availability and scope of
the statutory remedies. They are questions
which this Court is competent to examine under
Article 226.
75.20. The submission that the Petitioners, by
appearing before the Forest Settlement Officer,
submitted to his jurisdiction is also without
merit. A person who receives a proclamation
under Section 5 is called upon to present his
claim within the prescribed period. Section 9
attaches consequences to the failure to make a
claim within that period, subject to the
statutory provision enabling a person to
establish sufficient cause before the final
notification under Section 17. A person who
appears and places his documents before the
Forest Settlement Officer does so to protect the
rights asserted by him and to avoid the
consequences of failing to make a claim. Such
participation cannot, by itself, amount to
acceptance of the authority under which the
proceedings have been initiated.

- 389 -

75.21. More importantly, a defect going to the
jurisdiction of a statutory authority cannot be
cured by consent or acquiescence. If the
statutory foundation for an action is absent,
participation by an affected person cannot
supply it. The Petitioners' challenge to the legal
effect of the notification dated 05.02.1920 and
the Government Order dated 11.01.1924 is
evident from the pleadings. The Petitioners in
W.P. Nos. 16251/2023 and 19980/2023 have
challenged the proclamation dated 09.11.2023,
and the record in W.P. No. 16251/2023 also
shows that the Petitioner placed documents
before the Forest Settlement Officer in response
to the notice. Their participation does not
extinguish their right to question the legal
foundation of the proceedings.
75.22. The allegation of suppression is not established.
The learned Additional Advocate General relies
on the participation of some Petitioners before
the Forest Settlement Officer. He has not
identified any specific document or material fact
deliberately withheld by a Petitioner which, if
disclosed, would have affected the
consideration of the jurisdictional challenge.

The fact that a Petitioner participated in the
statutory inquiry does not, by itself, establish
suppression. The legal consequence of
participation, where an order determining a
claim has been passed, is a separate matter
governed by Section 16.
75.23. The order dated 24.10.2025 in W.P. No.
29656/2024 was passed after the writ petition
had been filed. Its non-disclosure at the time of
institution of the petition cannot be treated as
suppression. In any event, the order is not
under challenge in the prayers, and its merits
are not examined here. No Petitioner is denied
relief on the ground of suppression.
75.24. The learned Additional Advocate General also
submits that the Petitioners should participate
in the proceedings under Section 64A and allow
the competent authority to decide their claims.
Section 64A(3) provides an appeal to the State
Government or to an officer authorised by it
against an order passed by a Forest Officer
under Section 64A(1), within such period and in
such manner as may be prescribed. The
statutory appeal is against an order under

Section 64A(1), not a mere notice proposing
action.
75.25. The Respondents have not pressed the
existence of the appeal under Section 64A(3) as
a bar to the writ petitions in W.P. Nos.
16251/2023 and 19980/2023. Their submission
is that the Petitioners should participate in the
proceedings and place their documents before
the competent authority. The Petitioners, on the
other hand, question the authority to take
action under Section 64A, the statutory
conditions governing its exercise and the
competence of the officers who initiated the
proceedings. These are jurisdictional issues.
The existence of an appeal under Section
64A(3) does not, in the circumstances of these
cases, require this Court to decline examination
of those issues.
75.26. This Court has also considered whether the
petitions should nevertheless be declined in the
exercise of its discretion. The common
jurisdictional issue concerns the legal effect of a
notification issued in 1920 and the subsequent
Government Order dated 11.01.1924. The

proceedings have affected, or are asserted to
affect, a large number of landowners. The same
foundational question would otherwise arise
repeatedly before different authorities and
forums in individual proceedings. Its
determination by this Court avoids fragmented
consideration of a common question of law and
serves the interests of justice and orderly
administration.
75.27. The Hon’ble Supreme Court in M/s. Godrej
Sara Lee Ltd. (paragraph 4) has recognised
that, even where a sound legal point is raised,
the Court may decline relief if its grant would
not further public interest. That principle does
not justify refusal to examine the present
jurisdictional challenge. The question is
common to the connected petitions and
concerns the legal authority on which the State
has acted. The Respondents themselves
contend that the notification of 1920 and the
Government Order of 11.01.1924 must be
construed in their proper statutory and
historical context. That question has
accordingly been examined under the earlier
Points.

75.28. The principle laid down in Union of India v.
Kirloskar Pneumatic Co. Ltd. [(1996) 4 SCC
453, paragraph 10] and Manish Goel v.
Rohini Goel [(2010) 4 SCC 393, paragraph
14], that a writ court cannot direct a statutory
authority to act contrary to law, is not in
dispute. Entertaining the present petitions does
not require the Court to issue any such
direction. The Court examines the legal
foundation of the impugned action and leaves
the authorities free to exercise powers available
to them under an independent and applicable
statutory provision, subject to compliance with
the law.
75.29. The exercise of jurisdiction under Article 226 is
confined to the legal and jurisdictional
questions raised. It does not extend to deciding
disputed questions concerning title, the extent
or boundaries of individual parcels, the
character of the land on the ground, the validity
and effect of individual grants or transactions,
or the claim concerning movable articles
allegedly taken from the premises in W.P. No.
16251/2023. Those matters are left open for
determination before the competent authority

or the appropriate statutory forum, as the case
may be. The availability of those remedies does
not prevent this Court from examining the
common jurisdictional challenge or the legality
of the impugned action.
75.30. This Court answers Point No. (vii) by holding
that the Petitioners are entitled to invoke the
jurisdiction of this Court under Article 226 of
the Constitution. The availability of an
alternative statutory remedy does not render
the writ petitions unmaintainable. The Court
entertains the challenges to the legal
foundation of the impugned proceedings and to
the jurisdiction of the authorities to initiate or
continue them, as those challenges raise
questions of law falling within the recognised
exceptions to the rule of exhaustion of
alternative remedies. Section 16 of the
Karnataka Forest Act, 1963, provides an appeal
against orders determining claims under
Sections 11, 12 and 14, and Section 16(2)
provides an appeal against an order under
Section 15. It does not provide an appeal
against a notification, proclamation or notice.
The correctness of any order determining an

individual claim is not examined in these writ
petitions and remains subject to the remedy
under Section 16. The availability of an appeal
under Section 64A(3) does not, in the
circumstances of these cases, bar examination
of the jurisdictional challenge to the
proceedings under that provision. Participation
before the Forest Settlement Officer does not
amount to a waiver of the right to question the
statutory foundation of the proceedings, and
the allegation of suppression is not established.
The disputed questions of fact and the merits of
individual claims are left open for determination
in accordance with law.

provision was legally applicable to the
respective petition schedule properties;
whether the proceedings were initiated by

constituting a reserved forest. A notification
under Section 4 of the Mysore Forest
Regulation, 1900, is only the commencement of
that process and does not, by itself, authorise
dispossession. Until the statutory proceedings
are completed and a final notification under
Section 17 is issued, the lands cannot be
treated as reserved forest for the purpose of
invoking Section 64A of the Karnataka Forest
Act, 1963. He further submits that the
expression “deemed forest” cannot be used as
an independent source of authority to
dispossess persons in settled possession.
76.2. Sri Manmohan P.N. relies on Section 64A and
submits that the power of summary eviction
under sub-section (1) is available only in

76.10. On the status of the lands, he relies upon the
mutation said to have been effected in favour of
the Forest Department on 05.08.2013 in
respect of 536 acres and 22 guntas in
Achanahalli Village. He also refers to the
Records of Rights in which the Forest
Department's name is stated to appear in
Column Nos. 9 or 11 in respect of more than
6,400 acres. He submits that the absence of a
mutation entry in the Forest Department's
name for the remaining extent of Survey No. 92
does not establish that such land was excluded
from the notification.
76.11. Section 64A(1) of the Karnataka Forest Act,
1963, provides:
“Any person unauthorisedly occupying any land in
reserved forest, district forest, village forest,
protected forest and any other land under the
control of the Forest Department may, without
prejudice to any other action that may be taken
against him under any other provision of this Act or
any other law for the time being in force, be
summarily evicted, by a Forest Officer not below the
rank of an Assistant Conservator of Forests and any
crop including trees raised in the land and any
building or other construction erected thereon shall,
if not removed by him within such time as the
Forest Officer may fix, be liable to forfeiture:
Provided that before evicting a person under this
sub-section he shall be given a reasonable
opportunity of being heard.”

- 402 -
 HC-KAR

76.12. Section 64A confers a power of summary
eviction without requiring the Forest
Department to institute a civil suit. The power
is, however, confined to the categories of land
expressly specified in the provision. It is not a
general power to evict any person from land
which the Forest Department describes as
forest land. Its exercise is subject to distinct
statutory requirements.
76.12.1. First, the land must fall within one of the
categories specified in Section 64A(1).
76.12.2. Secondly, the person proceeded against
must be in unauthorised occupation of
that land.
76.12.3. Thirdly, the power must be exercised by
a Forest Officer not below the rank of an
Assistant Conservator of Forests.
76.12.4. Fourthly, the occupant must be given a
reasonable opportunity of being heard
before eviction.
76.13. These are conditions governing the exercise of
the power. The requirement of a reasonable
opportunity of hearing is expressly made

- 403 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685

WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
mandatory by the proviso to sub-section (1).
Section 64A(3) further provides an appellate
remedy to a person aggrieved by an order
passed under sub-section (1).
76.14. The first requirement is central to the present
controversy. Unless the land is shown to fall
within one of the specified categories, the
question whether the person occupying it is
unauthorised does not arise for determination
under Section 64A.
76.15. Applicability to the petition schedule properties.
Sri Manmohan P.N. is right in contending that
the classification of the land within one of the
categories specified in Section 64A(1) is a
jurisdictional requirement. The Respondents'
principal case is that the petition schedule
properties form part of the Moorkangudda
Block, which was notified on 05.02.1920 under
Section 4 of the Mysore Forest Regulation,
1900. Their claim is thus founded on the
notification and the proposal to constitute the
Block as a State Forest.
76.16. The Respondents do not assert that the petition
schedule properties were separately constituted

- 404 -
 HC-KAR

as district forest, village forest or protected
forest. They rely principally on the 1920
notification and, in the alternative, on the
expression “any other land under the control of
the Forest Department”. The applicability of
Section 64A must therefore be examined with
reference to those two grounds.
76.17. The first ground does not satisfy the statutory
requirement. This Court has held under Points
(i) to (iv) that the proposal contained in the
notification dated 05.02.1920 was withdrawn by
the Government Order dated 11.01.1924; that
the statutory process under the Mysore Forest
Regulation, 1900, was not completed by a final
notification under Section 17; and that no
subsisting notification under Section 4(1) of the
Karnataka Forest Act, 1963, or saved equivalent
has been established in respect of the Block.
76.18. Section 2(14) of the Karnataka Forest Act,
1963, defines “reserved forest” as land settled
and notified as such in accordance with Chapter
II of that Act. The Moorkangudda Block was not
constituted as a State Forest under the earlier
Regulation and did not acquire the status of a

- 405 -
 HC-KAR

reserved forest under the 1963 Act. The
notification of 05.02.1920, therefore, cannot
establish that the petition schedule properties
fall within the category of reserved forest for
the purpose of Section 64A(1).
76.19. The effect of Section 117 of the 1963 Act has
already been considered under Point (iii). That
provision does not revive the proposal
withdrawn in 1924. Nor can the Respondents
rely upon the proposition that land covered by a
preliminary notification necessarily retains the
status of forest land until the claims are
decided. That argument presupposes a
subsisting notification and has been dealt with
under Points (ii) and (iii). The 1920 notification,
by itself, does not bring the petition schedule
properties within any of the categories specified
in Section 64A(1).
76.20. The Respondents' alternative case is that the
lands constitute “any other land under the
control of the Forest Department”. This
category is distinct from reserved forest, district
forest, village forest and protected forest. It
may operate independently of a final

notification under Section 17. The absence of a
notification constituting reserved forest does
not, by itself, exclude the application of Section
64A to land which is otherwise established to be
under the control of the Forest Department.
The Respondents must, however, establish that
the particular land against which action is
proposed falls within that category.
76.21. The material relied upon by the Respondents
does not establish that requirement in respect
of the petition schedule properties. The
mutation dated 05.08.2013 is stated to relate
to 536 acres and 22 guntas in Achanahalli
Village. The Respondents also acknowledge the
absence of a corresponding entry for the
remaining extent of Survey No. 92. The
statement that the Forest Department's name
appears in the Records of Rights in respect of
more than 6,400 acres is an aggregate
assertion. It is not accompanied by a parcel-wise correlation establishing that the properties
against which the impugned proceedings were
initiated form part of that extent.

76.22. The presumption under Section 133 of the
Karnataka Land Revenue Act, 1964, applies to
qualifying entries in the Record of Rights and
certified revenue records, subject to rebuttal in
accordance with law. It operates in favour of
entries in the names of the Petitioners as well
as entries in the name of the Forest
Department. Such entries are relevant
evidence, but they do not, by themselves,
conclusively determine title or establish that a
particular parcel falls within a statutory
category under Section 64A. The legal effect of
the competing revenue records has been
considered under Point (v). The present
question requires the Respondents to establish
the statutory basis for invoking the power of
summary eviction against each property.
76.23. The impugned order dated 26.07.2022 is not
shown to have proceeded on a parcel-wise
determination that the lands were under the
control of the Forest Department. The material
relied upon by the learned Additional Advocate
General in these proceedings does not cure that
deficiency. It is not enough to refer generally to
Forest Department entries over a large extent

without establishing their application to the
particular properties against which action was
taken. This Court, therefore, does not hold that
no land in the Moorkangudda area can be under
the control of the Forest Department. It holds
only that the Respondents have not established
that the petition schedule properties fall within
that category on the basis on which the
impugned proceedings were initiated.
76.24. In W.P. No. 19980/2023, the Respondents rely
upon the Petitioner's description of his
occupation of Survey No. 92 of Achanahalli
Village as unauthorised and upon the rejection
of his applications for regularisation under the
Karnataka Land Revenue Act, 1964. The fact
that the Petitioner sought regularisation of his
occupation does not establish that the land is
reserved forest, district forest, village forest,
protected forest or other land under the control
of the Forest Department. The requirement
concerning the character and control of the land
under Section 64A(1) is independent of the
question whether the Petitioner's occupation is
authorised.

76.25. The record refers to the rejection of the
Petitioner's applications under Sections 94A and
94C of the Karnataka Land Revenue Act, 1964.
The Petitioner, on the other hand, states that an
order rejecting his application was passed by
the Assistant Commissioner on 05.06.2025 and
that his appeal is pending before the Deputy
Commissioner. Those proceedings are not
before this Court. No finding is made on their
validity, effect or present status. The Petitioner
does not acquire any right to remain in
occupation merely because the proceedings
under Section 64A cannot be sustained on the
basis advanced by the Respondents. The finding
is confined to the failure to establish the
jurisdictional condition under Section 64A(1).
76.26. The Petitioners' submission that Section 64A
can never be invoked until a final notification
under Section 17 is issued cannot be accepted
in that broad form. The provision is not
confined to reserved forests. It expressly
includes district forests, village forests,
protected forests and other land under the
control of the Forest Department. These
categories do not, in every case, depend upon

the issuance of a final notification under Section
17. The absence of such a notification is
therefore not, by itself, an answer to every
possible exercise of power under Section 64A.
76.27. The Petitioners succeed on the narrower and
decisive ground that the Respondents have not
established that their schedule properties fall
within any of the statutory categories. The
Respondents' reliance on Section 2 of the Forest
(Conservation) Act, 1980, and their submission
that the 1924 auction and subsequent grants
required the approval prescribed by that
provision concern the legal effect of the grants
and transactions. Those issues have been
considered under Point (v). The possible
application of the 1980 Act does not, without
more, establish that the land is under the
control of the Forest Department for the
purpose of Section 64A(1). This Court makes
no finding under this Point on whether any
individual parcel is forest land within the
meaning of the 1980 Act.
76.28. Competence of the officers. Section 64A(1)
requires the power of summary eviction to be

exercised by a Forest Officer not below the rank
of an Assistant Conservator of Forests. Section
2(6) of the Karnataka Forest Act, 1963,
includes a Deputy Conservator of Forests and
an Assistant Conservator of Forests within the
definition of “Forest Officer”. Section 2(3)
identifies the Deputy Conservator of Forests as
the Chief Forest Officer of a Forest Division. The
statutory rank of a Deputy Conservator of
Forests is higher than that of an Assistant
Conservator of Forests. Sri Manmohan P.N. also
accepts that position.
76.29. The order dated 26.07.2022 was passed by the
Deputy Conservator of Forests, Forest Mobile
Squad, Hassan. The order directed the
Assistant Conservator of Forests, Sakleshpur, to
carry out the eviction. Both officers satisfy the
minimum rank prescribed by Section 64A(1).
The rank requirement, therefore, does not
invalidate the order or the direction to the
Assistant Conservator of Forests.
76.30. The Petitioners' objection that the Deputy
Conservator of Forests lacked territorial
jurisdiction is not supported by any statutory

provision, rule or order placed before this

rank of the officer who may exercise the power.
No provision has been shown which excludes a
Deputy Conservator of Forests attached to the
Forest Mobile Squad from exercising that power
or imposes the territorial restriction asserted by
the Petitioners. In the absence of the relevant
statutory or administrative instrument, the
objection to territorial competence cannot be
accepted.
76.31. The position of the Range Forest Officer is
different. An officer below the rank of Assistant
Conservator of Forests cannot independently
exercise the power of summary eviction under
Section 64A(1). The notice dated 18.07.2023
was issued by the Range Forest Officer in
furtherance of the order dated 26.07.2022. It
cannot confer upon the Range Forest Officer a
power which the statute does not vest in him.
The notice also cannot survive independently of
the order pursuant to which it was issued.

76.34. In W.P. No. 29656/2024, the Assistant

invoking the power against the Petitioner's

that the property falls within any of the

notification dated 05.02.1920, which was
withdrawn in 1924. Nor has the Respondents'
alternative assertion that the property is under

jurisdictional objection to proceedings founded
on Section 64A is therefore made out.
76.35. Reasonable opportunity of being heard. The
proviso to Section 64A(1) is mandatory. It

requires that a person be given a reasonable
opportunity of being heard before he is evicted
under that provision. The requirement is not
satisfied merely by issuing a notice after the
decision to evict has already been taken. The
affected person must have a meaningful
opportunity to place his objections and
supporting material before the competent
officer, and those objections must be
considered before an order of eviction is made.
76.36. The Petitioners in W.P. No. 16251/2023 have
pleaded that, on 18.07.2023, officials entered
the resort and dwelling house without prior
notice and seized articles found therein. The
Respondents state that the resort was seized
and subsequently unsealed and restored.
76.37. The Respondents have not placed any material
before the Court to establish that notice was
issued to the Petitioners, or that they were
afforded an opportunity of being heard, before
the said action was taken. The material on
record, therefore, does not establish
compliance with the requirement of prior
notice.

76.38. Any fresh action under Section 64A must
strictly comply with the proviso to sub-section
(1). The person sought to be evicted must be
afforded a reasonable opportunity of being
heard before eviction. This is a mandatory
statutory safeguard and cannot be dispensed
with.
76.39. The remaining submissions require no separate

not examined, since the impugned action

already discussed and the present record does

The equities arising from grants, long

76.40. A separate issue arises in W.P. No. 29690/2025.

that the notices alleged contravention of

in Survey No. 76 of Kadumane Village.
76.41. Section 64 of the Karnataka Forest Act, 1963
was omitted by Act 1 of 1981 with effect from
23.02.1981. Section 64 and Section 64A are
distinct provisions. The omission of Section 64
does not, by itself, determine whether an
authority could have acted under another
provision of the Act, including Section 64A, if
the conditions of that provision were satisfied.

that Section 64A(1) of the Karnataka Forest

occupation of land falling within one of the

77. Answer to Point No. (ix): Proceedings before
the Forest Settlement Officer: In the writ
petitions challenging proceedings before the
Forest Settlement Officer, whether the
appointment of the Officer, publication of the
proclamation, service of notices, inquiry into

statutory provisions?

77.7. The challenge under this point concerns the
appointment of the Forest Settlement Officer,

publication of the proclamation, service of
notices, inquiry into claims and objections, and
determination of rights. These are stages of the
process prescribed under Chapter II of the
1963 Act. The first question is whether that
process could lawfully have been initiated in the
absence of the statutory foundation required by
the Act. The compliance of the individual steps
must be considered in that context.
77.8. Statutory foundation for proceedings before the
Forest Settlement Officer. Section 4(1) of the
1963 Act provides that, whenever the State
Government has decided to constitute any land
a reserved forest, it shall issue a notification
declaring that decision, specifying the situation
and limits of the proposed forest and, under
clause (c), appointing an officer, to be known as
the Forest Settlement Officer, to inquire into
and determine the existence, nature and extent
of the rights claimed in the land. Section 5
begins with the words, “When a notification has
been issued under section 4”, and requires the
Forest Settlement Officer to publish a
proclamation. Section 7, in turn, requires the

- 427 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024

WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
Officer to inquire into claims duly preferred
under Section 5.
77.9. The scheme is clear. The notification under
Section 4(1) is the statutory foundation for the
proceedings under Chapter II. The appointment
of the Forest Settlement Officer, publication of
the proclamation, receipt and inquiry into
claims, and determination of rights must follow
that notification. In its absence, the Officer
cannot invoke the powers conferred by those
provisions in relation to the land concerned.
77.10. It has already been held under Points (i) to (iii)
that the proposal contained in the 1920
notification was dropped by the Government
Order dated 11.01.1924; that Section 117 of
the 1963 Act did not revive or continue that
withdrawn proposal; and that no notification
under Section 4(1) of the 1963 Act has been
issued in respect of the Block. The Respondents
do not contend that such a notification was
issued. Their case is that the Forest Settlement
Officer was appointed by the Government Order
dated 19.12.2022 and that the subsequent

- 428 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
proceedings were taken on the basis of the
1920 notification.
77.11. The Government Order dated 19.12.2022,
cannot be treated as a substitute for the
notification expressly required by Section 4(1).
A separate order appointing an officer, even if
otherwise valid, does not declare the decision to
constitute the land a reserved forest, identify
the land in the manner required by the statute
or set in motion the process contemplated by
Chapter II. The appointment made by that
Government Order cannot, therefore, confer
upon the Officer jurisdiction to undertake
proceedings under Sections 5 and 7 in the
absence of the foundational notification under
Section 4(1). This conclusion does not amount
to a finding on the validity of the Government
Order for any other purpose. It is confined to its
inability to replace the statutory notification
required by Section 4(1).
77.12. Appointment and qualification of the Forest
Settlement Officer. Section 4(2) of the 1963 Act
prescribes the qualification and status of the
officer appointed as Forest Settlement Officer.

- 429 -
 HC-KAR

NC: 2026:KHC:63685

The distinction between a Forest Settlement
Officer and a Forest Officer is material. In
Emerald Haven Estates Ltd. v. State of
Karnataka, paragraphs 10 to 12, this Court
recognised that the inquiry into and
determination of rights under Chapter II are
entrusted to the Forest Settlement Officer. A
Forest Officer may represent the State in that
inquiry, but cannot assume the jurisdiction
vested in the Forest Settlement Officer.
Proceedings conducted by a Forest Officer in
place of the duly appointed Forest Settlement
Officer are without jurisdiction.
77.13. In W.P. No. 700/2026, the notice dated
30.06.2025, described by the Petitioner as a
notice under Section 7, is attributed to the
Deputy Conservator of Forests. The record does
not establish whether the notice was issued by
that officer in his capacity as a Forest Officer or
whether he acted as the Forest Settlement
Officer. No finding on that factual question is
necessary. If the notice was issued by a Forest
Officer acting in that capacity, the principle
stated in Emerald Haven Estates Ltd.,
particularly paragraphs 11 and 12, would apply.

- 430 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
If it was issued by the Forest Settlement
Officer, the absence of a notification under
Section 4(1) would deprive the proceedings of
their statutory foundation. The notice cannot be
sustained in either event, for want of the
notification required by Section 4(1).
77.14. Proclamation under Section 5. A proclamation
under Section 5 can be issued only after a
notification under Section 4(1). The
proclamations challenged in these petitions
were issued on the basis of the 1920
notification. As already held, the proposal
contained in that notification was withdrawn in
1924 and did not continue under the 1963 Act.
There was, therefore, no subsisting notification
under Section 4(1) on which the proclamations
could lawfully be founded.
77.15. Sri A.M. Vijay’s submission that the Forest
Department was required to assess the likely
impact of the proposed constitution of the
forest on the local population before issuing the
proclamation is not accepted as an independent
statutory requirement. Neither Section 4 nor
Section 5 of the 1963 Act expressly mandates a

prior assessment of that nature. No other
statutory provision or judicial authority
imposing such a requirement has been brought
to the notice of this Court.
77.16. The Act, however, provides for the protection
and adjudication of the rights of affected
persons through the claims contemplated by
Section 5, the inquiry under Section 7, the
exclusion of land in the circumstances specified
in Section 11(2)(i), and the right of appeal
under Section 16. The absence of a prior impact
assessment, by itself, does not establish a
violation of the Act.
77.17. The further contention that the proclamations
were issued without a decision under Section
4(1) is accepted to the extent that the
Respondents have not established that any
such decision was taken in accordance with that
provision. The absence of the required
notification is independently sufficient to
invalidate the proceedings. It is unnecessary,
therefore, to record findings on the allegations
of mala fides or breach of natural justice
founded on the same omission.

77.18. Service of notices. Section 5 requires the Forest
Settlement Officer, in addition to publishing the
proclamation, to serve a notice to the same
effect on every known or reputed owner or
occupier of any land included in, or adjoining,
the land proposed to be constituted a reserved
forest. This requirement is distinct from
publication of the proclamation. The statutory
obligation is to ensure that the persons
identified by the provision are individually
notified in the prescribed manner.
77.19. The Petitioners’ general assertion that no notice
was served requires consideration against the
pleadings in the individual petitions. In W.P. No.
16251/2023, the Petitioner states that he was
called upon by a proclamation notice to appear
on 04.12.2023 and that he produced his
documents. In W.P. No. 29656/2024, the
Petitioner challenges notices calling upon it to
appear and produce documents. These
pleadings establish that the Petitioners had
received or become aware of communications
requiring their appearance. They do not, by
themselves, establish that individual notices

- 433 -

were served on them in the manner required by
Section 5.
77.20. As regards the 87 Petitioners in W.P. No.
33767/2025 and the other Petitioners, the
record does not contain sufficient material to
determine, in each case, whether the statutory
requirement of individual service was complied
with. The Respondents have also not addressed
that question by placing the relevant service
records before the Court. No general finding on
actual service in each petition can, therefore,
be recorded. In view of the absence of a
notification under Section 4(1), the
proclamations and the proceedings founded on
them cannot be sustained, irrespective of
whether any individual Petitioner received a
notice.
77.21. Description and identification of the lands.
Section 4(1)(b) and Section 5(a) require the
situation and limits of the proposed forest to be
specified as nearly as possible. The Explanation
to Section 4(1) permits the limits to be
described by reference to roads, rivers, bridges
or other well-known or readily intelligible

boundaries. The statutory standard is one of
practical identification. The description must
enable affected persons to understand whether
their lands are included in the proposed forest
and to present their claims accordingly.
77.22. Sri A.M. Vijay submits that the survey numbers
mentioned in the proclamations and notices
have undergone changes through phodi,
durasthi and re-survey operations, making it
difficult for the affected persons to identify the
lands concerned. The changes in survey
numbers have also been considered under Point
(vi). This Court, therefore, does not record a
finding on the adequacy of the description
contained in any particular proclamation or
notice.
77.23. The issue nevertheless has direct relevance to
any fresh proceedings. A notification and
proclamation issued hereafter must describe
the proposed forest by reference to boundaries
and particulars that can be correlated with the
present survey records. Before calling upon any
owner or occupier to prefer a claim, the Forest
Settlement Officer must be satisfied that the

77.24. Inquiry into claims and determination of rights.
The Respondents submit that the Forest
Settlement Officer conducted inquiries and
passed orders under the 1963 Act. They also
state that claims were admitted wholly or in
part in certain cases. An inquiry under Section
7 and orders under Sections 11 to 14 are
subsequent stages of the process established
by Chapter II. Their authority depends upon the
existence of a valid notification under Section
4(1). Since no such notification was issued in
respect of the Block, the inquiry and orders
purportedly made in exercise of those
provisions lack the required statutory
foundation and cannot be sustained against the
Petitioners who challenge the proceedings.
77.25. It is unnecessary to examine whether any
particular claim was rightly admitted or
rejected, whether the evidence placed before
the Officer established title or any other right,
or whether the lands were correctly identified.
Those are matters which would arise for

have already been recorded under Point (vi).
Those admissions are unaffected by the present
conclusion.
77.26. The Petitioners’ submissions concerning the
stages prescribed by the Mysore Forest
Regulation, 1900, do not require a separate
determination under this point. Those
submissions bear on whether the Block was
ever constituted a State Forest under the
Regulation and whether the process
contemplated by that enactment culminated in
a notification under Section 17. Those questions
have been considered under Point (ii). The
proceedings initiated in 2023 and thereafter
were purportedly taken under the 1963 Act and
must be tested against the requirements of that
enactment. It is unnecessary to determine
under this point whether a proclamation was
published after the 1920 notification.

77.27. The conclusions recorded in this point do not
prevent the State from taking lawful steps to
protect the area or from initiating proceedings
to constitute any part of the Block as a
reserved forest. If the State decides to do so, it
must act in accordance with Sections 3 and
4(1) of the 1963 Act. That course requires a
fresh decision under the Act, a notification
identifying the land proposed to be constituted
a reserved forest and appointing a qualified
Forest Settlement Officer, followed by
compliance with the remaining provisions of
Chapter II. The State is at liberty to place
relevant ecological and other material before
the competent authority for consideration in
that process. No opinion is expressed on the
merits or sufficiency of such material.
77.28. This Court answers Point No. (ix) by holding
that the proceedings before the Forest
Settlement Officer are not in accordance with
the 1963 Act. A notification under Section 4(1)
is the statutory foundation for the proceedings
under Chapter II. No such notification was
issued in respect of the Block. The Government
Order dated 19.12.2022 appointing the Forest

Settlement Officer cannot substitute for that
notification. Consequently, the proclamations
issued under Section 5, the notices calling upon
the Petitioners to appear and prefer claims, and
the inquiries and orders founded on those
proceedings cannot be sustained.
77.29. The notice dated 30.06.2025 in W.P. No.
700/2026 is also liable to be quashed for want
of the notification under Section 4(1). Any fresh
proceeding to constitute land as a reserved
forest must commence in accordance with
Sections 4, 5 and 7 of the 1963 Act and must
comply with the other applicable provisions of
Chapter II.
78. Answer to Point No. (x): Orders under Section
14 and remedy under Section 16: In the writ
petitions challenging orders passed by the
Forest Settlement Officer under Section 14 of
the Karnataka Forest Act, 1963, whether the
Petitioners have an effective statutory appeal
under Section 16 before the Karnataka
Appellate Tribunal and, if so, whether the
circumstances of the respective cases
nevertheless warrant interference under Article
226 of the Constitution of India?

78.1. The submissions of Sri Manmohan P.N., learned
counsel for the Petitioners, on the objection
based on an alternative statutory remedy have
been considered under Point (vii) and need not
be repeated. In substance, he submits that the
existence of an appeal does not bar the
exercise of jurisdiction under Article 226 of the
Constitution; the rule requiring exhaustion of
alternative remedies is one of discretion and
not an inflexible rule of law. He contends that
the recognised exceptions apply because the
Petitioners challenge the jurisdiction of the
authorities to initiate proceedings on the basis
of the 1920 notification. He relies on M/s.
Godrej Sara Lee Ltd. v. Excise and Taxation
Officer-cum-Assessing Authority [(2025) 11
SCC 808], paragraphs 4 and 6, and Radha
Krishan Industries v. State of Himachal
Pradesh, [(2021) 6 SCC 771], paragraph 27.
As regards the remedy under the Mysore Forest
Regulation, 1900, he submits that no orders
were passed under Sections 10 to 14 of that
Regulation and, therefore, no occasion arose for
any claimant to invoke the appellate remedy
provided therein.

78.2. Sri Kiran Ron, learned Additional Advocate
General, submits that, in W.P. No. 29656/2024,
the Forest Settlement Officer passed an order
dated 24.10.2025 under Section 14 of the 1963
Act, recognising the Petitioner’s rights over 5
acres 38 guntas in old Survey No. 167,
corresponding to new Survey No. 102, of Agni
Village, while rejecting the claim in respect of a
further extent of 11 acres 18 guntas. He
submits that the Petitioner has an appeal under
Section 16 before the Karnataka Appellate
Tribunal against the rejection of the claim. The
Tribunal is competent to examine the legality
and correctness of the order, including the
treatment of the documents produced by the
claimant. According to him, the Petitioner
cannot seek reconsideration of the disputed
claim in a writ petition without first availing of
that statutory remedy.
78.3. In W.P. No. 33767/2025, Sri Kiran Ron submits
that 86 of the 87 Petitioners appeared before
the Forest Settlement Officer and that an
inquiry was conducted. A report was prepared,
and the claims of some Petitioners were
admitted wholly or in part. He submits that

those whose claims were rejected or admitted
only to a limited extent must pursue the
remedy of appeal under Section 16 before the
Karnataka Appellate Tribunal. Having
participated in the proceedings, they cannot
bypass that remedy and seek adjudication of
their claims in writ proceedings.
78.4. Section 16(1) of the 1963 Act provides:
“Any person who has made a claim under this
Chapter or any Forest Officer or other person
generally or specially empowered by the State
Government in this behalf, may, within three
months from the date of the order passed on such
claim by the Forest Settlement Officer under section
11, section 12, or section 14, appeal to the
Karnataka Appellate Tribunal and the decision of the
said Tribunal on such appeal shall be final.”
78.5. Under Section 16(1), a person who has made a
claim under Chapter II, a Forest Officer, or any
other person empowered by the State
Government may appeal to the Karnataka
Appellate Tribunal within three months from the
date of an order passed by the Forest
Settlement Officer on a claim under Section 11,
Section 12 or Section 14. The decision of the
Tribunal on such appeal is final.

78.6. Section 11 deals with claims to rights in or over
land. Section 12 deals with claims relating to
rights of way, pasture, forest produce and
water. Section 14 deals with the exercise of
rights admitted by the Forest Settlement
Officer. The Respondents describe the order
dated 24.10.2025 in W.P. No. 29656/2024 as
one passed under Section 14.
78.7. It is unnecessary, for the present purpose, to
determine whether the claim concerning rights
in land ought to have been dealt with under
Section 11 rather than Section 14. Section
16(1) expressly provides an appeal against an
order under any of those three provisions.
78.8. Accordingly, a person aggrieved by an order of
the Forest Settlement Officer on a claim under
Section 11, Section 12 or Section 14 has a
statutory right of appeal to the Karnataka
Appellate Tribunal within the period prescribed
by Section 16(1). This remedy is confined to
orders of the nature specified in that provision.
It does not provide an appeal against the initial
proclamation under Section 5, the appointment

- 443 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024

WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
of the Forest Settlement Officer or a notice
merely calling upon a person to prefer a claim.
78.9. The remedy under Section 16(1) is effective for
challenging the merits of an order passed on a
claim. A claimant whose claim has been
rejected, or whose claim has been admitted
only to a lesser extent than claimed, may seek
appellate examination of the material relating
to the existence and extent of the asserted
rights, including the evidence concerning title
and possession. These are primarily matters of
fact. The High Court, in exercise of its writ
jurisdiction, does not ordinarily undertake the
role of an appellate forum for reappraising such
evidence.
78.10. In M/s. Godrej Sara Lee Ltd., paragraph 4,
the Hon’ble Supreme Court reiterated that a
High Court should “normally not entertain a writ
petition, where an effective and efficacious
alternative remedy is available”. In Radha
Krishan Industries, paragraph 27.5, the
Hon’ble Supreme Court held that, where a
statute creates a right and prescribes the
remedy or procedure for enforcing that right or

- 444 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
liability, the statutory remedy must ordinarily
be pursued. Paragraph 27.6 also recognises
that the existence of disputed questions of fact
may be a ground for declining to exercise writ
jurisdiction.
78.11. The learned Additional Advocate General is,
therefore, correct to the extent that a claimant
who seeks reconsideration of the extent of the
rights recognised by the Forest Settlement
Officer, or challenges the rejection of a claim on
its merits, must ordinarily pursue the appeal
provided under Section 16(1). This Court does
not undertake any such reappraisal in these
petitions.
78.12. The further question is whether the
circumstances of these cases nevertheless
warrant interference under Article 226 of the
Constitution. The answer depends on the
nature of the challenge and the relief actually
sought in each petition.
78.13. None of the six petitions seeks a direct
adjudication by this Court on the merits of an
individual order passed on a claim under
Section 11, Section 12 or Section 14. The

principal challenge is to the jurisdictional
foundation of the proceedings themselves,
namely, the authority to initiate proceedings
under Chapter II of the 1963 Act on the basis of
the 1920 notification, despite the withdrawal of
the proposal in 1924 and the absence of a
notification under Section 4(1) of the 1963 Act.
The distinction between a challenge to the
jurisdiction to initiate the proceedings and a
challenge to the merits of an individual claim is
material.
78.14. The order dated 24.10.2025 in W.P. No.
29656/2024 was passed after the writ petition
had been instituted. No amendment was made
to the petition to challenge that order. In W.P.
No. 33767/2025, the relief sought includes a
prayer to quash the proclamations and notices
and, consequently, all further proceedings taken
pursuant to them. The effect of that prayer
requires separate consideration.
78.15. In W.P. No. 29656/2024, the order dated
24.10.2025 is not directly under challenge. This
Court neither sets aside that order nor
examines the correctness of the decision

recognising 5 acres 38 guntas and rejecting the
claim to the further extent of 11 acres 18
guntas. It would be inappropriate to grant relief
against an order that the Petitioner has not
specifically challenged and whose merits have
not been placed before this Court for
adjudication.
78.16. The Petitioner’s remedies against that order,
including the remedy under Section 16(1),
remain governed by law. Any question
concerning limitation or the availability of a
particular remedy must be determined by the
competent forum. At the same time, the order
dated 24.10.2025 cannot, by itself, validate the
proceedings initiated without the notification
required by Section 4(1). Nor can the State rely
upon that order, or the proceedings of which it
forms part, to disturb the Petitioner’s
possession otherwise than in accordance with
law. This protection is within the scope of the
relief sought in the petition and shall be
reflected in the operative part.
78.17. The position in W.P. No. 33767/2025 is
different. Prayer (i) seeks to quash the

proclamations and notices and, consequently,
“all further proceedings pursuant thereto”. The
inquiry conducted by the Forest Settlement
Officer, the report prepared in those
proceedings and the orders, if any, passed on
the claims of the Petitioners are subsequent
steps arising from the impugned proclamations
and notices. They are founded on the same
proceedings whose statutory basis has been
challenged.
78.18. The jurisdictional defect identified in this
judgment is anterior to the consideration of
individual claims. No notification under Section
4(1) of the 1963 Act was issued in respect of
the Block. The proclamations and the
subsequent proceedings under Chapter II were,
therefore, initiated without the statutory
foundation required by the Act. The orders, if
any, passed upon the claims in those
proceedings cannot be severed from that defect
and sustained as independent determinations
under Chapter II.
78.19. Section 16(1) provides an appeal against an
order passed on a claim under Section 11,

Section 12 or Section 14. It does not provide a
direct appeal against the proclamation under
Section 5, which is the foundational step
challenged in these petitions. The issue decided
by this Court is not whether the Forest
Settlement Officer correctly assessed the
evidence or properly determined the extent of
any individual claim. It is whether the
proceedings in which those determinations were
made had the statutory foundation required by
Section 4(1). That common jurisdictional issue
has been decided in these writ petitions.
78.20. In these circumstances, the inquiry, report and
orders passed on the claims of the Petitioners
in W.P. No. 33767/2025 are liable to be set
aside as consequential relief within the scope of
prayer (i), insofar as they concern the
Petitioners and their respective lands. This
conclusion does not involve an adjudication of
the merits of any claim or a determination of
title, extent or possession.
78.21. Setting aside the consequential proceedings in
W.P. No. 33767/2025 does not take away any
benefit arising from the State’s specific

statement that it will not proceed against the
lands to the extent of the admitted claims of

statement, already recorded under Point (vi),
binds the Respondents and shall be reflected in

78.22. The setting aside of the proceedings leaves the
Petitioners in the position they occupied before
those proceedings commenced. It does not
confer or declare title in their favour. If the
State initiates any fresh proceedings under the
applicable law, the Petitioners may present their
claims in those proceedings, and the State may
contest them in accordance with law. Any order
made on such claims will carry the statutory
remedies available under the 1963 Act.
78.23. The submission that participation in the
proceedings precludes the Petitioners from
challenging their jurisdictional foundation has
already been considered under Point (vii).
Participation in proceedings does not, by itself,
cure the absence of statutory jurisdiction or
take a case outside the recognised exceptions
to the rule requiring exhaustion of alternative

remedies. The principles stated in M/s. Godrej
Sara Lee Ltd., paragraph 6, and Radha

the reasons recorded under that point.
78.24. The Court is not directing the Petitioners to
pursue appeals against orders which are being
set aside as consequential steps in proceedings
initiated without the statutory foundation
required by Section 4(1). At the same time, no
Petitioner is deprived of the right to pursue the
statutory remedy against an order on the
merits of a claim made in any fresh proceeding
lawfully initiated under the 1963 Act. Such a
remedy, where available, will be governed by
Section 16.
78.25. This Court answers Point No. (x) by holding that
an appeal lies under Section 16(1) of the 1963
Act to the Karnataka Appellate Tribunal, within
three months, against an order of the Forest
Settlement Officer passed on a claim under
Section 11, Section 12 or Section 14. It is an
effective statutory remedy for challenging the
merits of such an order, including the

adjudication of the merits of individual claim
orders. The challenge is to the jurisdictional
foundation of the proceedings under Chapter II,
namely, the absence of a notification under
Section 4(1) of the 1963 Act. The Petitioners
are, therefore, not relegated to the remedy
under Section 16 in respect of that challenge.
78.27. In W.P. No. 29656/2024, the order dated

79. Answer to Point No. (xi): Proceedings of the
Regional Commissioner: In the writ petitions

of the Regional Commissioner, Mysuru Division,
whether those proceedings possess any binding

validity or continuation of the forest
proceedings?

of the Forest and Revenue Departments of
Hassan District. They contend that the
meetings recognised that the Government of
Mysore had withdrawn the proposal initiated by
the notification of 1920 in 1924.

dropping of cases against them. The
consideration of such representations does not
establish that the competent authority had
taken a decision to terminate the proceedings.
79.5. He further submits that the State Government
has not issued any order directing that the
proceedings be dropped. A decision required to
be taken by a competent authority must be
evidenced by an order made in accordance with
law. It cannot be inferred merely from an oral
statement or an understanding said to have
emerged at a committee meeting. According to
the learned Additional Advocate General, the
Regional Commissioner did not issue any such
direction. Annexure-L, therefore, does not
confer any legal right on the Petitioners, and, in
the absence of an authenticated decision of the
competent authority, the Respondents were
entitled to continue the proceedings.
79.6. The Petitioners have placed before this Court
the minutes of the coordination meeting of

Revenue and Forest Department officials,
chaired by the Regional Commissioner, Mysore

Assistant Commissioner, Sakleshpur, concerning
the Moorkangudda issue.
79.7. The minutes record that directions were issued
to the Deputy Conservator of Forests
(Territorial) and the Range Forest Officer to
stop prosecution cases against members of the
public and farmers in the area. They also record
that:
“[C]lear directions were issued by the Regional
Commissioner, Mysore Division to wait for the
directions from the Government of Karnataka on the
issue of Moorkangudda section 4 notification”.
79.8. The minutes further state that the Tahsildar and
the Assistant Commissioner were directed to
search for old grant documents and submit a
report to the Government.
79.9. The minutes of the meeting held on
08.09.2022. It states that information
concerning the cancellation of the
Moorkangudda Forest Block, which the minutes
place in the year 1923, was furnished to the
Deputy Conservator of Forests (Territorial), the

Assistant Conservator of Forests and the Range
Forest Officer. It further records that documents
said to establish the cancellation, including
Gazette publications and a certified copy of the
original file obtained from the Department of
Archives, were handed over to the Forest
Department.
79.10. The minutes then state:
“[D]irections have been issued to the Deputy
Conservator of Forest (Territorial), Hassan District,
Assistant Conservator of Forest, Sakleshpur and
Range Forest Officer, Sakleshpur to withdraw all the
forest offence cases filed against the people and
farmers on the basis of section 4 of Moorkangudda
block within 15 days”.
79.11. The proceedings do not bind the State
Government. The notification of 1920 was
issued by the Government, and the proposal
contained in it was withdrawn by the
Government Order dated 11.01.1924. The legal
effect of that order has been determined under
Point (i) on the basis of the original
Government records. The proceedings of the
Regional Commissioner cannot alter that
determination or operate as a decision of the
State Government to withdraw, revive or
otherwise modify the legal position.

79.12. No statutory provision or Government order has
been brought to the notice of this Court
conferring on the Regional Commissioner the
authority to cancel, declare cancelled or revive
a notification issued by the Government under
the forest law. The minutes of the meeting
dated 10.06.2022 themselves record that the
Regional Commissioner directed the officers to
await the Government’s decision on the issue.
The record does not disclose any subsequent
Government order pursuant to those minutes.
The learned Additional Advocate General is,
therefore, correct in submitting that the
proceedings cannot be treated as a decision of
the State Government.
79.13. The proceedings also do not establish a legally
binding direction capable of invalidating the
statutory proceedings initiated by the Forest
Department or of creating an enforceable right
in favour of the Petitioners against that
Department. The statement in the minutes
dated 08.09.2022 that directions had been
issued to withdraw forest offence cases is not
supported by a separate authenticated order of
the Regional Commissioner. More importantly,

- 458 -
 HC-KAR

the Petitioners have not identified any statutory
provision or rule under which such a direction
would operate to terminate proceedings
initiated under the Karnataka Forest Act, 1963,
or render them invalid.
79.14. The 1963 Act confers the relevant statutory
powers on the authorities specified in its
provisions. The validity of the exercise of those
powers must be tested against the Act and the
applicable law. It cannot be determined merely
by reference to an administrative
communication recorded in the minutes of a
coordination meeting.
79.15. Whether the officers concerned were required,
as a matter of internal departmental discipline
or administrative hierarchy, to act upon any
direction issued at the meeting is not necessary
for deciding these writ petitions. No finding is
recorded on that question.
79.16. The proceedings of the Regional Commissioner
do not determine the validity or continuation of
the forest proceedings. Those questions fall to
be decided on the basis of the applicable
statutory provisions and the original

- 459 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024

Government records. As held under Points (i) to
(iv), the proposal contained in the 1920
notification was withdrawn by the Government
Order dated 11.01.1924; that proposal was not
revived by Section 117 of the 1963 Act; and
the State cannot sustain the impugned
proceedings on the strength of the withdrawn
proposal. The proceedings challenged in these
petitions fail for the reasons recorded under
those points and not because of any direction
attributed to the Regional Commissioner.
79.17. The learned Additional Advocate General is
correct in submitting that the meetings, in the
absence of an authenticated decision of the
competent authority, did not by themselves
require the Respondents to treat the
proceedings as terminated. That conclusion,
however, does not validate the proceedings on
their merits or provide an independent
statutory foundation for their continuation.
Their validity must stand or fall on the
applicable law, as determined in the preceding
points.

79.18. This Court has examined the original archival
records in determining the legal effect of the
1920 notification and the Government Order
dated 11.01.1924. The minutes of the meetings
refer to the original file and Gazette
publications said to have been obtained from
the Department of Archives. They describe the
cancellation as having taken place in 1923,
whereas the Government order in the archival
record is dated 11.01.1924.
79.19. The reference in the minutes to the year 1923
and the legal opinion recorded therein do not
determine the date or legal effect of the
Government action. Similarly, the Assistant
Commissioner’s report dated 11.10.2022
expresses an opinion on the effect of the
historical documents. Such an opinion, whether
made by an officer of the Revenue Department
or otherwise, cannot substitute for an
examination of the Government records and the
applicable law. The conclusion under Point (i)
rests on the original archival documents and
the legal effect of the Government Order dated
11.01.1924. Neither the minutes of the
Regional Commissioner nor the Assistant

Commissioner’s report is the basis of that
finding.
79.20. The allegation in W.P. No. 19980/2023 that
Respondents Nos. 3 and 4 were present when
the direction was issued is not disputed. Their
alleged presence, however, does not cure the
absence of an authenticated order or confer on
the proceedings the legal effect claimed by the
Petitioners.
79.21. The Petitioners’ challenge to the forest
proceedings does not depend on the legal effect
of the Regional Commissioner’s proceedings.
The conclusions under Points (i) to (iv) rest on
the Government Order dated 11.01.1924, the
statutory scheme and the absence of a lawful
basis for continuing the proceedings on the
withdrawn proposal. The Petitioners obtain the
relief flowing from those findings. The
proceedings of the Regional Commissioner may
have been an administrative effort to address a
dispute affecting landowners and residents in
the area. They do not, on the material
produced, constitute a source of enforceable

legal rights in favour of the Petitioners or a
ground for invalidating the forest proceedings.
79.22. This Court answers Point No. (xi) by holding
that the proceedings of the Regional
Commissioner, Mysuru Division, dated
10.06.2022 and 08.09.2022, do not have
binding legal effect on the State Government or
the Forest Department so as to terminate or
invalidate the proceedings under the Karnataka
Forest Act, 1963. They do not alter the legal
effect of the Government Order dated
11.01.1924 or the conclusions recorded under
Points (i) to (iv). The Assistant Commissioner’s
report dated 11.10.2022 and the legal opinion
recorded in the minutes are opinions on the
effect of the historical documents. This Court
rests no finding on those opinions. The validity
of the impugned forest proceedings is
determined by the original Government records
and the applicable statutory provisions.
80. Answer to Point No. (xii): Individual rights and
relief: Whether, in the facts and circumstances
of each writ petition, the Petitioners have
established a legally enforceable right

warranting the relief sought, including any
challenge to eviction, sealing of premises,
seizure of articles or other consequential
action?
80.1. Sri Manmohan P.N., learned counsel for the
Petitioners, submits that the lands in question
were granted or auctioned by the Revenue
Department to the Petitioners or their
predecessors-in-title. According to him, such
grants and auctions must be given effect unless
set aside in accordance with law. The Forest
Department cannot disregard the grants and
the revenue records and assert jurisdiction over
the lands on the basis of the 1920 notification.
80.2. He further submits that purchasers of lands
sold by auction subsequently obtained orders of
conversion for non-agricultural use, including
for establishing resorts, secured the licences
and registrations required for their businesses
and invested substantial sums. The authorities
failed to consider these circumstances before
taking action against them. He also contends
that persons in settled possession cannot be

80.3. Sri A.M. Vijay, learned counsel for the
Petitioners in W.P. No. 29690/2025, submits
that the eviction of the Petitioners in W.P. No.
16251/2023 violates Article 300A of the
Constitution. He relies on the grants made by
the State in Kadumane Village from 1952
onwards as conduct consistent with the
withdrawal of the 1920 notification. He also
relies on the revenue records, which, according
to him, have stood in the names of the owners
for several decades without any entry showing
the Government or the Forest Department as
the owner. He submits that the entries carry the
presumption contemplated by Section 133 of
the Karnataka Land Revenue Act, 1964, and
that the owners have regularly paid land
revenue and taxes. He further contends that
revival of the notification after nearly a century
would cause serious prejudice to persons who
have invested in houses, resorts and
plantations.

80.4. Sri Kiran Ron, learned Additional Advocate
General, submits that the Petitioners have not
established any legally enforceable right over
the lands. They have not produced the source
of their predecessors’ title or established a
complete chain of title. In W.P. No. 29656/2024,
the purchase was made on 19.01.2011, nearly
nine decades after the 1920 notification. A
purchaser cannot acquire a better title than
that held by the transferor.
80.5. In W.P. No. 16251/2023, the learned Additional
Advocate General submits that, even assuming
that the auction of 1924 conferred a valid right,
that right extended only to 5 acres. The
Petitioners are alleged to have extended their
occupation and construction to a further extent
of 9 acres 36 guntas.
80.6. In W.P. No. 19980/2023, the Petitioner has
himself described his occupation as
unauthorised in his applications for
regularisation, which have been rejected.
80.7. In W.P. No. 33767/2025, the learned Additional
Advocate General relies on the claims admitted
by the Forest Settlement Officer, the State’s

statement that it will not proceed against the
admitted extents of Petitioners Nos. 13, 14, 15,

80.8. The learned Additional Advocate General further
submits that revenue entries do not confer title
where the underlying right has not been validly
created. The Revenue Department could not
grant land under the control of the Forest
Department. Grants or assignments made after
the commencement of the Forest
(Conservation) Act, 1980, must also satisfy
Section 2 of that Act. He contends that there
can be no promissory estoppel against the
performance of statutory duties.
80.9. He relies on Union of India v. Kirloskar
Pneumatic Co. Ltd., (1996) 4 SCC 453,
paragraph 10, and Manish Goel v. Rohini
Goel, (2010) 4 SCC 393, paragraph 14, to
submit that this Court cannot direct public
authorities to act contrary to law. According to
him, the Petitioners cannot obtain an order
restraining the authorities from enforcing the

forest laws merely on the basis of revenue

80.10. This point requires the Court to determine the
nature of the rights established by the
Petitioners and the relief that follows in each
case. It is necessary to distinguish between the
right to challenge State action taken without
lawful authority and the right, if any, to the land
itself.
80.11. A Petitioner need not establish title to obtain
relief against State action founded on a
notification that has no legal force. Equally, the
absence of a lawful basis for the particular
action challenged does not establish the
Petitioner’s title or confer a right to retain land
irrespective of the applicable law. The validity
and extent of the grants, auctions, transfers,
conversions and other transactions, as well as
the precise extent and character of each
Petitioner’s interest, are matters of evidence.
They are not decided in these proceedings. This
is consistent with the findings under Points (v)
and (vi).

80.12. The Petitioners have established a legally
enforceable right not to have their possession
or asserted interests disturbed by executive
action founded solely on the 1920 notification.
The proposal contained in that notification was
withdrawn by the Government Order dated
11.01.1924. It was not revived by Section 117
of the 1963 Act, and no notification under
Section 4(1) of that Act has been issued in
respect of the Block.
80.13. Article 300A of the Constitution provides:
“No person shall be deprived of his property save by
authority of law.”
80.14. The protection under Article 300A requires the
State to deprive a person of property only
under the authority of valid law and in
accordance with the procedure prescribed by
that law. Insofar as a Petitioner establishes a
proprietary interest, the State cannot deprive
that person of the property by relying on a
proposal that was withdrawn and has not been
replaced by a valid statutory notification.
80.15. A person in occupation who has not established
title cannot, merely by reason of possession,

- 469 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024
NC: 2026:KHC:63685
WP No. 29656 of 2024
C/W WP No. 16251 of 2023
WP No. 19980 of 2023
AND 3 OTHERS
claim ownership or an indefeasible right to
remain on the land. But the State must still act
under an applicable legal provision and follow
the procedure prescribed by it. The present
judgment does not confer title, regularise any
unauthorised occupation or protect any person
against lawful eviction. It protects the
Petitioners against coercive action founded on
the withdrawn 1920 proposal and against
deprivation otherwise than in accordance with
law. That is the enforceable right which
warrants the relief considered below.
80.16. The submissions of the Respondents based on
Union of India v. Kirloskar Pneumatic Co.
Ltd. and Manish Goel v. Rohini Goel are
accepted as statements of law. In paragraph 10
of Kirloskar Pneumatic, the Hon’ble Supreme
Court held that the power under Articles 226
and 227 is:
“designed to effectuate the law, to enforce the rule
of law and to ensure that the several authorities and
organs of the State act in accordance with law”
and:
“cannot be invoked for directing the authorities to
act contrary to law”.

- 470 -
 HC-KAR

CNR: KAHC010629462024

WP No. 19980 of 2023

80.17. In paragraph 14 of Manish Goel, the Hon’ble
Supreme Court held that:
“no court has competence to issue a direction
contrary to law nor can the court direct an authority
to act in contravention of the statutory provisions”.
80.18. These principles do not preclude the relief
granted in the present cases. This Court does
not direct the Forest Department to recognise
any grant, regularise any occupation, permit
any activity prohibited by law or refrain from
enforcing the forest laws. The direction is
confined to preventing action on the basis of a
notification whose proposal was withdrawn, and
which has no continuing legal force. The State
remains at liberty to take action under any
independently applicable statutory provision,
provided that its requirements are established
and the prescribed procedure is followed. Such
relief gives effect to the law; it does not
restrain its lawful enforcement.
80.19. The principle in Jit Ram Shiv Kumar v. State
of Haryana [(1981) 1 SCC 11], paragraph 51,
that promissory estoppel cannot operate
against the exercise of legislative power or the
discharge of statutory duties, is also accepted,

- 471 -

as considered under Point (v). The relief
granted here does not rest on promissory
estoppel or on any assurance given by a State
officer. It rests on the absence of a lawful
foundation for the particular action challenged.
80.20. The grants, auctions, conversions, licences,
registrations and investments relied upon by
the Petitioners do not form the basis for
granting relief in these proceedings. Their
validity and legal effect are not decided. The
Respondents’ contentions that the Revenue
Department lacked competence to make certain
grants, and that grants or diversions made after
25.10.1980 must satisfy Section 2 of the Forest
(Conservation) Act, 1980, are likewise not
decided, for the reasons recorded under Point
(v).
80.21. The equities arising from the Petitioners’
investments and the ecological concerns relied
upon by the State may be placed before the
competent authority in any fresh proceedings
lawfully initiated under the applicable law.
Neither those equities nor the ecological value
of the area can dispense with the statutory

requirements governing the exercise of State

respective lands. The relief is consequential to
the absence of the notification required by
Section 4(1) of the 1963 Act. No finding is
made on the merits of the individual claims or
on the Petitioners’ title.
80.45. The A.Mo. series notices challenged under
prayer (ii) require separate treatment. They
cannot be sustained to the extent that they
seek to proceed solely on the basis of the
withdrawn 1920 notification. This judgment
does not, however, invalidate any proceeding
that may be founded on an independent
statutory power, the applicability of which has
not been examined in these petitions. Any such
proceeding must satisfy the requirements of the
law under which it is initiated.

80.48. Section 133 of the Karnataka Land Revenue
Act, 1964, attaches a rebuttable presumption of
truth to entries in the Record of Rights and
certified entries. Such entries do not, by
themselves, confer title. Their evidentiary effect
is also not confined to the Petitioners’ case; the
presumption must be considered in relation to
the relevant entries and the material relied
upon to rebut it.

cannot assume that every such entry was made

can the Court direct wholesale deletion without

80.52. The notice dated 30.06.2025 is quashed for
want of the notification under Section 4(1) of
the 1963 Act. The Respondents shall not initiate
or continue action against the Petitioner on the
basis of the withdrawn 1920 notification. This
does not prevent action under an independently
applicable statutory provision, provided its
requirements are established and the
prescribed procedure is followed. No finding is
made on the Petitioner’s title or on the location
of the lands in relation to the boundaries
referred to in the 1920 notification.

forest, it may do so by following Sections 3 and

in Chapter II. It must take a fresh decision,
issue the notification required by Section 4(1),

reserved forest and appoint a qualified Forest
Settlement Officer. The claims and rights of
affected persons must thereafter be dealt with
in accordance with the Act.
80.54. The State may also take action against
unauthorised occupation under Section 64A or
any other applicable provision, provided that
the land falls within the category to which that
provision applies, the statutory conditions for
its exercise are established and the competent
authority follows the prescribed procedure. In
particular, any action under Section 64A must
satisfy the requirements concerning the nature
of the land, unauthorised occupation, the rank
of the Forest Officer exercising the power and
the reasonable opportunity of hearing required
by the proviso to Section 64A(1).
